There was silence for some moments. The man fastened the ends of the rawhide strips to a peg in the floor and began to braid them. At length he said, “Where is the boy; he hasn’t been in all the morning. When do you want him to go?”

“He might as well go now, today, the sooner the better. Of course he’s down by the creek with his little bow and arrows.”

“Well, wife, I wish you would go and call him. I don’t want these strips to dry on me while I am braiding them.”

The woman went to the banks of the little stream that ran by the village, and called in a shrill voice, “Oo-maʻ-a-be! Oo-maʻ-a-be!”

“I’m coming!” shouted a bareheaded, black-eyed little boy, just as he shot a blue-joint grass arrow at a frog that had poked his head above the surface of the water to see what was going on in the outer world. Forgetting the call, the lad went stealthily on up the stream with another arrow strung, looking for other frogs that might be hunting for flies or mosquitoes, or enjoying the kisses of the warm sunshine in some pleasant nook.

===Chinese===
沉默了片刻。男人把生牛皮条的一端拴在地板的木钉上，开始编起来。末了他说：“孩子在哪儿？一上午都没在家。你想让他什么时候去？”

“越早越好，如今就去，今天就走。他准是带着小弓箭在小河边上。”

“那么，孩子他妈，劳你去叫叫他。我不愿编着编着，这些皮条在我手里干了。”

女人来到从村边流过的小溪岸上，尖声喊道：“Oo-maʻ-a-be！Oo-maʻ-a-be！”

“来啦！”一个光着头、黑眼睛的小男孩应声喊道，恰在这当口，他把一支蓝节草箭射向一只把脑袋探出水面、看看外面世界在闹什么的青蛙。喊声忘在了脑后，这少年又搭上一支箭，蹑手蹑脚沿溪而上，寻找别的青蛙——也许正捕着苍蝇蚊子，或在哪处舒坦的角落里享受暖阳的亲吻。

===Original===
“What can the boy be doing?” said the woman to herself, then she called again, this time emphasizing the first syllable of the name to indicate that she was losing patience, “Ooʻ-ma-a-be!”

With reluctant steps the boy made his way toward his mother, peering as he went into the tall grass to see if a grasshopper or any other creature might be exposing itself to the arrows of a sport-loving lad.

“Why did you not come when I first called you?” asked the woman as she took the child by the hand and led him with quickened steps toward the little house.

As the mother and son entered, the father looked up with a pleasant smile, and addressing the boy said, “Your mother went to call you because she wants us to go to the house of the White-chests, where you are to stay and learn to write. Now wash your hands and face, and make yourself look nice, so they will be pleased with you; then we will go.”

The mother had the water ready, and began scrubbing the face and neck of the lad, while the candidate for scholarship was pressing his lips tightly together and squinting his eyes to exclude the soap that persisted in getting into them. Then followed the brushing of the hair, which was equally irksome to the boy, and he unconsciously leaned farther and farther away until he was pulled to again by the fond parent.

When both face and hair shone, the mother kissed her boy and announced to her husband that the child was ready. The father rose to go with him, but the boy held back.

“What is it?” asked the father; “are you not willing to go?”

“I am willing to go,” answered Oo-maʻ-a-be, “but I want to put on my embroidered moccasins and leggings and my little buffalo robe.”

The husband and wife looked at each other smiling, and let the youngster have his own way, so he was decked out in his gorgeous costume. He folded himself up in his robe, which was beautifully ornamented with porcupine quills of exquisite colors, he twisted his body and neck to see if he looked well, then said he was ready to go.

===Chinese===
“这孩子在干什么呢？”女人自言自语道，接着又喊，这一回把名字的头一个音节喊得格外重，表示她要沉不住气了：“Ooʻ-ma-a-be！”

男孩老大不情愿地朝母亲挪过去，一路还往高草里张望，看有没有蚱蜢或别的什么活物，好送到爱玩的少年的箭下。

“我一喊你，你怎么不来？”女人一面问，一面拉起孩子的手，加快脚步朝小屋走去。

母子俩进门时，父亲抬起头，愉快地一笑，对孩子说：“你母亲去叫你，是因为要我们一起去白胸人家里；你要住在那儿学写字。现在去把手脸洗干净，把自己拾掇得体体面面，叫他们看了欢喜；然后我们就走。”

母亲早已备好水，动手给孩子擦脸擦脖子；这位未来的学者紧闭嘴唇、眯缝双眼，防着那偏要往眼睛里钻的肥皂。接着是梳头，男孩同样不胜其烦，不知不觉把身子越仰越远，又被疼爱他的母亲一把拽了回来。

等脸和头发都发出光来，母亲亲了亲孩子，对丈夫说孩子准备好了。父亲起身要带他去，男孩却退缩不前。

“怎么了？”父亲问；“你不愿意去吗？”

“我愿意去，”Oo-maʻ-a-be 答道，“可我要穿上绣花的鹿皮鞋和裹腿，还有我的小野牛皮袍。”

夫妻俩相视而笑，随这小家伙自便，于是他一身华服，装扮停当。他把身子裹进袍子里——那袍子用色彩精美的豪猪鬃缀得漂亮极了——又扭了扭身子脖颈，看看自己好不好看，然后说可以走了。

===Original===
In the schoolroom a class of big boys and girls were learning to read in concert:﻿—

> “The boy stood on the burning deck,

 Whence all but he had fled.”

Again and again the teacher made them read the lines, but each time someone would either lag behind or read faster than the others. While this was going on I was busy with my spelling lesson, as my class came after the one now hard at work with the boy “on the burning deck.”

There was a click; I raised my eyes and looked toward the door; it slowly opened, then a tall man and a boy silently entered. I recognized them at once; the man was a friend of my father and the lad one of my playmates on my weekly visits home. The class on the floor was dismissed with a lecture on reading, and Graybeard turned to call, “Next class,” when he discovered the man and boy sitting on a bench near the door.

===Chinese===
教室里，一队大男孩大女孩正齐声朗读：——

> “那男孩站在燃烧的甲板上，

> 除他之外，人皆已逃散。”

老师让他们一遍又一遍地读这两行诗，可每回总有人落在后头，或者比别人读得快。这当口我正忙着拼写功课，因为我们班排在眼下正跟“燃烧的甲板”上的男孩较劲的那一班后面。

咔哒一声响；我抬起眼朝门口望去；门缓缓开了，一个高个子男人和一个男孩悄没声地走了进来。我一眼认出了他们；那男人是父亲的朋友，男孩是我每星期回家时的玩伴之一。台前那班人挨了一通关于朗读的训话被打发回座，灰胡子正转身要喊“下一班”，却发现了坐在门边长凳上的父子俩。

===Original===
“How do you do, Wa-honʻ-e-ga?” said Graybeard, approaching the Indian with outstretched hand.

“*Ka-gaeʻ-ha!*”3 responded the Indian, his face brightening. Then in a low tone he called me to him and said, “I have brought your grandfather here to stay with you. Be as good to each other as you have always been, and try to learn the language of the White-chests.”

The boy was a distant relative, and, following the peculiar system of kinship among the Indians, there was no impropriety in my addressing him as my grandfather, although we preferred to call each other friend.

“What does Wa-honʻ-e-ga want?” asked Graybeard, putting his hand on my shoulder.

===Chinese===
“你好，Wa-honʻ-e-ga？”灰胡子伸着手朝那印第安人迎上去。

“Ka-gaeʻ-ha（朋友）！”3那印第安人应道，脸上放出光来。接着他压低声音把我叫到跟前，说：“我把你爷爷带来了，让他跟你住在一处。你们要像往常一样彼此相待，用心学白胸人的话。”

那男孩是个远亲；按照印第安人特有的亲属制度，我管他叫爷爷并无不妥，不过我们更愿意彼此称朋友。

“Wa-honʻ-e-ga 有什么贵干？”灰胡子把手搭在我肩上问。

===Original===
“My friend,” replied the Indian, looking with a kindly smile into the face of the teacher, “my wife wishes her son, this boy, to learn to speak the language of the Big-knives,4 so I have come with him. We have brought him up with great care, and I think he will give you no trouble.”

“Tell him,” said Graybeard, “I am very glad he has brought the boy, and we will do our best for him.”

The Indian turned and with silent dignity left the room.

“Now, children,” said Graybeard, taking out the school register and looking at us, “we have a new boy here, and we must select a good name for him; what have you to suggest?”

We promptly called him Edwin M. Stanton, and he was registered by that name.

Brush and I were detailed to take Edwin to the storeroom and fit him with a new suit of clothes. When he was dressed; we tied up his fine Indian costume in a neat bundle to be returned to his father.

===Chinese===
“我的朋友，”那印第安人答道，含着和蔼的笑望进老师的脸，“我妻子想让她的儿子，就是这个孩子，学会讲大刀子（Big-knives）4的语言，所以我带他来了。我们一直把他精心带大，我想他不会给你添麻烦。”

“告诉他，”灰胡子说，“他把孩子送来，我很高兴，我们一定尽力照管他。”

那印第安人转过身，带着无声的庄重离开了教室。

“好了，孩子们，”灰胡子取出学籍簿，望着我们说，“我们这儿来了个新同学，得给他挑个好名字；你们有什么提议？”

我们当即给他取名埃德温·M·斯坦顿（Edwin M. Stanton），学籍簿上就登记了这个名字。

布拉什和我被派领埃德温去库房，给他配一身新衣裳。他穿戴好了，我们把他的漂亮印第安装束打成整整齐齐的一个包袱，好还给他父亲。

===Original===
At the supper-table Edwin and I sat together. I showed him how to bow his head when the blessing was asked, and to turn his plate. He silently followed my whispered instructions, and was very quiet while supper was going on, but during the religious exercises which followed, when we dropped on our knees, he became very anxious to know why we did so. He shuffled a good deal in his position, and after a while stood up and looked around. I pulled him down, and he demanded out loud, “What are we hiding for? This is the way we do when we are hiding in the grass.”

I gave him a good dig in the ribs. “That hurts!” he cried. I whispered to him to be quiet, but before long he was fidgeting again. Just as the superintendent lowered his voice at an earnest passage in his prayer Edwin spoke out again, in a louder tone than before, “I’ve got a dog; he can catch rabbits!”

Graybeard lifted his head, and the superintendent paused in his fervent appeal and looked toward us; he rapped with his knuckles on the table, and said, in a severe tone, “Boys, you must be silent and listen when I pray.”

I whispered to Edwin that he must keep still until we got out.

As we were going to bed that night Edwin said, “*Ka-gaeʻ-ha*,5 let you and me sleep together; I don’t want to sleep with anyone else.”

===Chinese===
晚饭桌上，埃德温和我坐在一起。我教他祷告时该怎样低头，怎样转盘子。他默默地照我的耳语行事，晚饭间一直安安静静；可随后的礼拜一开始，我们跪下去时，他却急切地想知道我们干吗这样。他在原地蹭来蹭去，过了一会儿干脆站起来四下张望。我把他拽下来，他大声发问：“我们藏什么呢？我们在草里藏身的时候就是这样的。”

我狠狠捅了他肋骨一下。“疼！”他叫起来。我悄声叫他安静，可没多久他又坐立不安了。校监祷告到恳切处、把声音压低的那一瞬，埃德温又开了口，嗓门比先前还大：“我有一条狗；它会逮兔子！”

灰胡子抬起了头；校监在火热的祈求中停住，朝我们这边望来；他用指节敲了敲桌子，声色俱厉地说：“孩子们，我祷告的时候，你们必须安静听着。”

我悄悄告诉埃德温，出去之前千万别出声。

那晚我们上床睡觉时，埃德温说：“Ka-gaeʻ-ha5，你跟我睡一头吧；我不想跟别人睡。”

===Original===
Lester too wanted to sleep with me; so it was arranged among us that Brush and Warren should have the double bed, and Edwin, Lester, and I were to have the wide bed for three.

After we had settled down, Edwin began talking, “When we finished eating,” he said, “we turned around and the old man began to talk, then you all sang. I like to hear you sing; you’ve got a good voice. Then we went down on our knees, just as though we were hiding in the grass; what did we do that for? The old man talked a long time; was he telling a story? I know a great many of them; I know one about a dog. He was a man, but he was turned into a dog. I’ll tell it to you.”

I didn’t say anything, so Edwin began:

“Far back in the earliest times there dwelt in a little village a man and his wife. They had only one child living, a son whom they loved to adoration. He was so handsome a youth that whenever he walked through the village all eyes were turned upon him with admiration. One day he asked his mother to make him a separate tent. When it was done he went into it, and there spent four days and nights in solitude, neither eating nor drinking. Then he came out and spoke to his father and mother and said, ‘I am going away to be gone a long time, perhaps never to return. I go to meet the White-swan, the magician who sent my brothers to the abode of shadows, and, in conflict, with magic opposing his magic, I will destroy him or die as my brothers have died.’ The father and mother, remembering the fate of their other children, wept and pleaded with their son not to leave them, but he was determined to go.

===Chinese===
莱斯特也想跟我睡；于是我们几个商定，布拉什和沃伦睡那张双人床，埃德温、莱斯特和我睡那张三人宽床。

我们躺定之后，埃德温开了腔：“吃完了饭，”他说，“我们转过身去，那老人就开始讲话，然后你们大家唱歌。我爱听你们唱；你嗓子真好。后来我们都跪下去，就像藏在草里似的；那是干什么呀？那老人讲了老半天；他在讲故事吗？我知道的故事可多了；我知道一个关于狗的。他本来是个人，后来变成了狗。我讲给你们听。”

我没吭声，于是埃德温讲了起来：

“在最早最早的年代，一个小村里住着一个男人和他的妻子。他们只有一个活着的孩子，是个儿子，疼爱得像心尖子。那青年生得太俊了，每回他从村里走过，所有人的目光都带着赞叹追着他。有一天他求母亲给他单立一顶帐篷。帐篷立好了，他住了进去，独自在里面待了四天四夜，不吃不喝。然后他出来对父亲和母亲说：‘我要出一趟远门，也许永远不回来。我去会那天鹅法师（White-swan），是他把我哥哥们送进了阴影之居；我要跟他斗法，以法破法，不杀死他，就像哥哥们那样死去。’父母想起其他孩子的下场，哭着恳求儿子别丢下他们，可他去意已决。

===Original===
“The young man travelled many days, when one morning he beheld a maiden sitting on the brow of a hill. He went to her and asked why she sat there all alone. Without lifting her eyes, modesty forbidding her to return his gaze, the maiden replied, ‘I go to marry Hi*n*-hpeʻ-ah-gre.’ The youth was seized with fear lest the young woman might be the White-swan transformed to beguile him; but being struck by her maidenly bearing, and becoming enamoured of her beauty, he turned aside from suspicion and permitted himself to be persuaded that the fair creature before him was in reality one of his own kind. And so he spoke and said, ‘I am he, Hi*n*-hpeʻ-ah-gre, the man whom you seek to follow.’ In reply the maiden said, ‘It makes my heart throb with delight to meet and to see with my own eyes the man I am to marry. Sit down and rest your head in my lap, and when the weariness of travel has left you, I shall follow you wherever you may lead.’ Joy filling the heart of the youth, and no longer troubled with misgivings, he laid his head upon the lap of the maiden and soon fell fast asleep.

“ ‘Tha! Tha!’ exclaimed the woman, using a word of magic, and four times, in quick succession, she pulled the ears of the young man. He awoke with a start and attempted to rise, but a transformation had taken place, instead of a man standing upright, he found himself to be a four-footed beast. His body had changed, but his reason was still that of a man. He turned to see his companion, and lo! he beheld, not the beautiful maiden in whose lap he had fallen asleep, but one who looked down upon him with contempt, and whom he knew to be the White-swan. The thought that he had been outwitted came to the young man like a flash, and as swiftly his magic word returned to his mind. He tried to utter it, but he only yelped and gave a dismal howl like that of a dog. A cringing, mangy, lop-eared dog, he now followed the White-swan and﻿—Are you asleep?”

I was almost asleep, so I did not answer him, then he became silent. When I awoke Edwin was gone; I called him but he did not answer. Brush and I went downstairs and called softly in the schoolroom, but the boy was not there, then we went to the large door of the hall and found it unbolted. We returned to the dormitory and went to bed, and I soon fell asleep again.

Toward morning I was awakened by strange sounds on the stairs leading up to our dormitory. I recognized the footsteps of a human being, but there were other footsteps that were like those of a four-footed beast. They approached my bed; they came near, and a voice said in Indian in a loud whisper, “Lie down, lie down!”

===Chinese===
“那青年走了许多天，一天早晨，他望见一位少女坐在山冈顶上。他走上前去，问她为何独自坐在这里。少女没有抬眼——羞怯不许她回望他的目光——答道：‘我要去嫁 Hi*n*-hpeʻ-ah-gre。’青年心里一惊，唯恐这少女正是天鹅变形来诱他上钩；可他被她少女的端庄打动，又迷恋上她的美貌，疑心便抛在了一边，让自己相信眼前这美丽的生灵真是自己的同类。于是他开口说：‘我就是你要找的人，Hi*n*-hpeʻ-ah-gre，你要跟随的丈夫。’少女答道：‘遇见我该嫁的人，亲眼看见他，我的心欢喜得直跳。坐下吧，把头枕在我膝上歇歇；等你旅途的疲乏散了，你到哪里，我就跟到哪里。’喜悦充满青年的心，疑虑尽消，他把头枕在少女的膝上，很快沉沉睡去。

“‘Tha！Tha！’那女人喊出咒语，接着飞快地、一连四次揪那青年的耳朵。他猛地惊醒，想站起来，可变化已经发生：站着的不再是直立的人，他发觉自己成了一头四条腿的牲畜。身子变了，理智却还是人的。他转身去看同伴，看哪！在他眼前俯视他的，不是他枕着她的膝睡去的那位美丽少女，而是一个轻蔑地朝下望着他的人——他知道，这正是天鹅。‘我中计了’——这念头像闪电一样掠过青年的心，他的咒语也同样飞快地回到记忆里。他想喊出来，可发出的只是尖叫和一声狗似的凄厉长嚎。他成了一条夹尾巴、生癞疮、耷拉耳朵的狗，从此跟着天鹅——你们睡着了没有？”

我差不多睡着了，没有答他，他也就不作声了。我一觉醒来，埃德温不见了；我喊他，他不应。布拉什和我下楼到教室里轻声喊，孩子不在；我们又去大厅的大门前，发现门没有上闩。我们回到宿舍上床，我很快又睡着了。

临近清晨，通向我们宿舍的楼梯上奇怪的声响把我弄醒了。我听出有人的脚步，还夹杂着四足牲畜似的脚步声。它们朝我的床走来；到近处，一个声音用印第安语大声低语道：“趴下，趴下！”

===Original===
“Is it you, Oo-maʻ-a-be?” I asked.

“Yes, I’ve been after my dog,” he answered, getting into bed with his clothes on.

“Get up and undress; you can’t sleep with your clothes on! What did you go after the dog for?”

“I wanted you to see him, and I thought we’d keep him here. He is a fine dog; he can swim too!”

“But were you not afraid? It was dark.”

“I forgot all about being afraid, and I went right by that big grave too﻿—the one they say a ghost comes out of and chases people. I ran, though, all the way to my house. The dog was lying near the door; he was so glad to see me he almost knocked me down.”

It was nearly morning, and we went right off to sleep. Suddenly we were aroused by a furious barking. Brush, Edwin, and I sprang out of bed, and rushed for the dog that with legs spread was defending the top of the stairs.

===Chinese===
“是你吗，Oo-maʻ-a-be？”我问。

“是我，我去追我的狗了，”他答道，和衣钻进被窝。

“起来把衣裳脱了；哪能穿着衣裳睡！你干吗去追狗？”

“我想让你看看他，还想着咱们把他养在这儿。他是条好狗；还会凫水呢！”

“可你不害怕吗？天那么黑。”

“害怕早忘光了，我还打那座大坟边上直直走过去——就是他们说有鬼出来撵人的那座。不过我一路跑到了我家。狗就卧在门口；他见了我欢喜得差点把我撞倒。”

天快亮了，我们倒头便睡。忽然一阵狂吠把我们惊醒。布拉什、埃德温和我跳下床，扑向那条叉开腿守住楼梯口的狗。

===Original===
“Boys, what have you up there?” called Graybeard from the foot.

“Edwin went after his dog last night,” answered Brush. “He wants to keep it here.”

“He does, eh! Will it bite?”

“No, it won’t bite; you can come up.”

===Chinese===
“孩子们，上头是什么东西？”灰胡子在楼梯脚下喊。

“埃德温昨夜去把他的狗接来了，”布拉什答道。“他想把它养在这儿。”

“哦，是嘛！它咬人吗？”

“不咬；你上来吧。”

## VIII Fraudulent Holidays / 骗来的假日

===Original===
“Third Reader,” called Graybeard, and some ten or twelve boys and girls marched to the place of recitation, and put their toes on a straight crack in the floor. The reading lesson was some verses on “Summer,” prettily illustrated with a picture of a boy and a dog, the lad racing over a meadow, and the dog frisking at his side.

“Now, Robert, begin!” said Graybeard to little Bob, who in some unaccountable way had reached the head of the class.

The boy put his index finger on the first word, and slid it along as he read, in a low, singsong tone, “Come, come, come, the Summer now is here.”

“Read that over again,” said Graybeard. “Read it loud, as though you were out of doors at play.”

===Chinese===
“三级读本，”灰胡子喊道，十来个男孩女孩列队走到背诵处，脚尖抵住地板上一道笔直的裂缝。阅读课的课文是几行咏“夏天”的诗，还配着一幅好看的插图：一个男孩和一条狗，男孩奔过草场，狗在他身边欢跳。

“现在，罗伯特（Robert），开始！”灰胡子对小鲍勃（Bob）说——他不知怎么竟排到了全班头名。

那男孩把食指按在第一个词上，一面读一面顺着字滑过去，用低低的、唱经似的调子念：“来呀，来呀，来呀，夏天现在已经来到。”

“再念一遍，”灰胡子说。“念响亮点，就像你在户外玩耍时那样。”

===Original===
Bob read again, but in the same manner, and had hardly gone through half the line when the sharp crack of Graybeard’s ruler on the desk made us all jump.

“That’s not the way to read it!” he exclaimed with some impatience; and he repeated the lines to show how they should be given. “Now, begin again.”

Bob began, but in the same lifeless tone, never taking his finger from the words.

“Next!” interrupted Graybeard. “The same verse; read as though you were wide awake and calling to your playmates, not as if you were going to sleep.”

The boy addressed straightened himself up and shouted out:

===Chinese===
鲍勃又念了一遍，还是那副腔调，一行没念到一半，灰胡子的戒尺啪的一声敲在课桌上，把我们全都吓了一跳。

“不是这么念的！”他有些不耐烦地喊道；他把那几行诗示范了一遍，好让他们知道该念成什么样。“现在，重新开始。”

鲍勃开始了，还是那副死气沉沉的调子，手指头没离开过字。

“下一个！”灰胡子打断他。“还是这一节；念得像你完全醒着、在喊你的玩伴那样，别像要睡着似的。”

被点到名的男孩挺直身子，大声喊了出去：

===Original===
“Come, come, come, the Summer now is here!” going through the verse without a break, then he glanced proudly toward the girls, only to see them giggling behind their books.

“Silence!” cried Graybeard, striking his desk. “That was well done!”

The door slowly opened, and the farmer entered, hat in hand, and addressed Graybeard, “I want to transfer a sow with a litter of pigs from one pen to another, and I’ve come to ask if you could let me have the help of some of the boys?”

When permission had been granted, a number of willing hands went up, and as many faces turned with eager expectancy to the farmer, who looked around, and then said, “Brush, Frank, Lester, and Warren will do.”

===Chinese===
“来呀，来呀，来呀，夏天现在已经来到！”他一口气念完了这一节，然后骄傲地朝女孩们瞟了一眼，却只见她们躲在书本后面咯咯直笑。

“肃静！”灰胡子拍着桌子喊。“念得好！”

门缓缓开了，农场工人手里拿着帽子走进来，对灰胡子说：“我想把一头母猪和一窝小猪从一个圈挪到另一个圈，来问问你能不能让我借几个男孩帮帮忙？”

得到许可后，好多只手热切地举了起来，好多张脸满怀期待地转向农场工人；他四下看了看，说：“布拉什、弗兰克、莱斯特和沃伦就行。”

===Original===
We followed the farmer to the pen, and at once jumped in, each one seizing a little pig; but, before we could turn, the sow made such an onslaught upon us that we dropped the pigs and scrambled over the fence; but Lester, who was last, left a piece of his trousers in the jaws of the angry beast. After this exciting experience, at which the farmer could hardly stop laughing, we held a consultation with him, and agreed upon a plan which we immediately proceeded to carry out.

We threatened the sow with our hats; she retreated into a corner with her young; then Brush slyly went up, and, reaching his hand through the fence, caught one of the little pigs by the legs and held it fast; it squealed lustily, and the infuriated mother made savage attacks upon the fence. Then Lester, Warren, the farmer, and I sprang into the pen, caught the frightened little pigs, and ran with them to the new pen. Brush released his prisoner, and the cry of the transported little ones brought the mother to the pen, where she was secured.

While the farmer was fastening the gate, we boys walked around the hog-yard; Warren, who was ahead, discovered a weak place in the fence, and beckoned excitedly for us to hasten.

There were times when the pupils became very tired of their books, and longed to take a run over the prairies or through the woods. When this longing came upon them, they sought for ways and means by which to have the school closed, and secure a holiday. I remember once, it was in the fall, the members of the Big Seven loosened the joints of the long stovepipe during recess. When school opened in the afternoon, and their class was called, they marched to the place of recitation, keeping step and jarring the room so that the sections of the pipe fell rattling to the floor, filling the room with smoke, and covering floor and desks with soot. As it would take some time for the pipe to cool and be put up again, and the room cleaned, the school was dismissed, giving us a half holiday.

===Chinese===
我们跟着农场工人到了猪圈，立刻跳了进去，一人逮住一头小猪；可我们还没来得及转身，那母猪就朝我们猛扑过来，我们扔下小猪，连滚带爬翻过栅栏；垫后的莱斯特在怒兽的口中留下一截裤腿。这场让农场工人笑得直不起腰的惊险经历过后，我们同他合计了一番，定下一个马上动手执行的计划。

我们拿帽子吓唬那母猪；她带着猪崽退进角落；然后布拉什悄悄凑上去，把手从栅栏缝里伸进去，一把攥住一头小猪的腿；小猪拼命尖叫，暴怒的猪妈妈凶猛地冲击栅栏。这时莱斯特、沃伦、农场工人和我跳进猪圈，逮住惊慌的小猪，把它们抱进新圈。布拉什放了他的俘虏，被搬走的小猪崽的叫声把母猪引到圈里，她一到就被关住了。

农场工人拴圈门的时候，我们几个男孩在猪场里溜达；走在前头的沃伦发现栅栏有处不结实，激动地招手叫我们快过去。

学生们也有对书本厌倦到极点、巴不得去草原上或树林里跑一圈的时候。这种渴望一来，他们就千方百计让学校停课，好捞个假日。我记得有一回，是在秋天，大七人的成员趁课间休息把长烟囱管的接口弄松了。下午开课、轮到他们班时，他们踏着步子走向背诵处，故意把屋子震得直颤，烟囱管一节节哗啦啦砸在地板上，满屋子灌满烟，地板和课桌落满黑灰。烟囱管要放凉、重新装好，屋子要打扫，都得费些工夫，于是放了学，我们白捡了半天假。

===Original===
Now, in the weakness of the hog fence, there was a chance for an afternoon out of school, and Warren saw it. He told us his plan, and the rest of us fell in with the scheme. After dinner we took some corn and scattered it outside of the fence at the weak place; then we went to the schoolroom, where Graybeard, when he came to ring the bell to summon the scholars, found us hard at work on our arithmetic lessons.

The geography class was up, and Brush was describing the rivers of South America, when the door was thrown open by the superintendent, who exclaimed, “Hurry, boys! The pigs are out and going to the Indians’ cornfield!”

We did not wait to be ordered a second time; but, snatching our hats from the pegs in the hall, we ran down the hill with wild shouts and cries. All the afternoon we chased pigs, and had a glorious time, while the girls had to stay in school and be banged at by Graybeard.

It was almost suppertime when we finally drove the pigs into the yard and repaired the weak places in the fence. Flushed with our exciting chase we entered the dining-room when the bell rang, and took our places at the table for the evening meal; then the superintendent, looking at us with a kindly smile, thanked us for the good service we had rendered that afternoon!

===Chinese===
如今，猪圈栅栏那处不结实，正是捞一个下午不上学的机会，沃伦一眼就看出来了。他把计划讲给我们听，其余的人全赞成。吃过午饭，我们弄了些玉米，撒在栅栏不结实处的外面；然后进了教室；灰胡子来摇铃召集学生时，看见我们正埋头用功算术呢。

地理课上场了，布拉什正在讲南美洲的河流，门被校监猛地推开，他喊道：“快，孩子们！猪跑出来了，正往印第安人的玉米地去！”

我们没等吩咐第二遍；从大厅挂钩上抓起帽子，喊着叫着冲下山坡。整个下午我们追猪，玩得痛快极了；女孩们却只好待在学校里挨灰胡子的敲打。

等我们终于把猪赶进猪场、把栅栏不结实的地方修好，天都快开晚饭了。我们追得满面红光，铃一响就进了饭厅，在桌边各就各位吃晚饭；校监望着我们，慈祥地笑着，感谢我们那天下午出的力！

===Original===
The few hours’ release from the tasks of the schoolroom had brought about a general good feeling among the boys; so, when we had partaken of the simple fare, we gathered on our playground and joined in a number of lively games in the long twilight. So interested and excited had we become in our play that we took no notice of the fading light and the lateness of the hour until the first bell for bed sounded.

Our school was an industrial one, and in the assignment to the larger boys and girls of various duties in and about the building, I was given the care of the hydraulic ram that pumped the water from the spring to the house. In the morning I started it, and in the evening shut it off. The ram was located in a wooded ravine a quarter of a mile from the school, and I usually stopped it while it was yet light, for, like many a foolish boy, I was afraid to go away from the house alone in the dark. Now in the excitement of play I had forgotten all about the ram until I heard the bell calling us to prepare for bed, nor had I realized till then that it was dark, and that the sky in the west was black with storm clouds through which the lightning zigzagged, and that there was an incessant rumble of thunder. The myriad of fireflies that filled the air with flashes of red light only made the darkness seem yet darker.

Stricken all at once with fear, I called loudly for Brush and the rest of the boys, but none of them responded. I was afraid to go to the ram alone in the dark, but if I should let it pump all night the water would overflow the kitchen, and that would mean a disgraceful punishment for me. I went from boy to boy, trying to secure a companion; but not one of them dared to go with me, they were all afraid of ghosts. Marbles could not tempt them, nor could a much coveted gunlock, which for the first time I was willing to part with, induce any boy to go. The time for the last call for bed was fast approaching, and I dared not wait longer trying to secure an escort, so I started on a run, frightened nearly out of my wits at everything I saw, but on I went as if racing for life.

I reached the place and stood over the square pit in which the ram was placed, and was about to go down the ladder into it, when I saw something move rapidly at the bottom. I nearly fell over backwards as I jumped away. I ran toward the house, but the thought of the overflow in the kitchen, and the punishment that was sure to follow, came back to my mind. For a moment I struggled between a known and an unknown fate, and decided to meet the latter. With set teeth and clenched fists I jumped into the pit, backed into the nearest corner, yelled at the top of my voice, while I struck right and left with my fists and kicked out with my feet. Let it be ghost or beast, I was determined to fight it and die game. I kept on striking, kicking, and yelling, but nothing put itself in my way. I dropped to the ground, panting, but kept an eye on the white thing which had also moved into a corner. I made a feint at charging upon it and it fled to another corner; then I put my head close to the ground to discover the shape of my enemy, when, to my joy, I discerned the outline of a rabbit. With a long-drawn breath of relief I stood upright, turned off the ram, made a rush upon the rabbit and caught it. Hastily rolling it up in my jacket, I climbed the ladder, ran up the hill as though a dozen ghosts were after me, and reached my bed just in time to say “Amen” to the evening prayer.

===Chinese===
这几个小时摆脱功课，让男孩们普遍心情大好；于是吃罢便饭，我们聚到操场上，在长长的暮色里玩起一场又一场快活的游戏。我们玩得那样入迷、那样起劲，竟没留意天光暗下来、时辰不早了，直到就寝的第一遍铃响。

我们学校是所工读学校；在给大点的男孩女孩分派楼里楼外各项差事时，派给我的是照管那台把泉水打进屋里的水锤泵。早晨我开动它，傍晚我关掉它。水泵安在离学校四分之一英里的一条林木繁茂的沟谷里，我通常趁天还亮就把它停了，因为像许多傻男孩一样，我怕天黑一个人离开屋子。这回玩得兴起，我把水泵忘得一干二净，直到听见铃喊我们去准备睡觉才想起来；也是到那时我才发觉天已经黑了，西边的天空乌云滚滚，闪电在里面曲曲折折地窜，雷声隆隆不绝。满天飞舞的萤火虫用一道道红光划破空气，反倒把黑暗衬得更黑。

我猛然害怕起来，大声喊布拉什和其余的男孩，可没有一个应声。我不敢黑着天一个人去水泵那儿，可要是让它抽一整夜，水就会漫进厨房，那等着我的就是丢人的惩罚。我挨个儿男孩去求，想找个伴儿；可谁也不敢陪我去，他们都怕鬼。弹珠打动不了他们，连我头一回舍得割爱的那把人人眼馋的枪机，也诱不动哪个男孩。就寝最后一次点名眼看就到，我不敢再磨蹭着找护送的了，只好撒腿就跑，看见什么都吓得魂飞魄散，还是拼命地跑，像逃命一般。

我跑到了地方，站在安着水泵的方坑上头，正要顺着梯子下去，忽然看见坑底有什么东西飞快地动了一下。我吓得倒退一步，几乎仰面摔倒。我朝屋子跑，可一想到厨房漫水、随后必定挨罚，又转了念头。已知与未知两种命运之间，我挣扎了片刻，决定迎着后者去。我咬紧牙、攥紧拳跳进坑里，背靠进最近的角落，扯着嗓子大喊，两拳左右开弓，双脚乱踢。管它是鬼是兽，我横下心跟它拼了，死也要死得硬气。我又是打、又是踢、又是喊，却没有任何东西撞上来。我瘫在地上直喘气，眼睛盯住那团也缩进角落的白东西。我虚晃了一记冲锋，它逃进另一个角落；我把头贴到地上，想看清敌人的形状——叫我大喜过望的是，我认出那轮廓是只兔子。我长长地松了口气，站直身子，关掉水泵，朝兔子猛扑过去把它逮住了。我慌忙用外套把它一裹，爬上梯子，跑上山坡，活像后面跟着一打鬼，赶到床上时正好赶上晚祷的“阿们”。

===Original===
When Graybeard had gone down, I whispered to Lester and Edwin, “I’ve caught a white rabbit!”

“Let’s scare the boys,” said Lester.

So we dropped the little creature on the floor, and it ran around the room as hard as it could go, while one of us cried out in a loud whisper “Ghost!” Then every boy in the room pulled the bedclothes over his head, and did not dare to uncover again.

We kept the rabbit for a pet, and made a box for it. We liked to watch it eat, and it did not suffer for want of food so long as we had it. One of the gangs among the small boys came to us one day while we were feeding our pet, and offered us some clay marbles for it. We looked upon their offer with contempt, for we all knew how to make clay marbles ourselves, and had all we wanted.

===Chinese===
灰胡子下楼后，我悄悄告诉莱斯特和埃德温：“我逮着一只白兔！”

“咱们吓吓男孩们去。”莱斯特说。

于是我们把那小东西往地上一放，它撒开腿满屋乱跑，我们中的一个大声低呼：“鬼！”满屋的男孩个个把被子蒙过头顶，再不敢露出来。

我们把兔子养着玩，还给它做了个箱子。我们爱看它吃东西；只要它在，它就不缺吃的。一天我们正喂这宝贝，小男孩里的一伙人来到跟前，要用几颗泥弹珠跟我们换。我们对这出价嗤之以鼻，因为泥弹珠我们自己都会做，要多少有多少。

===Original===
“I’ll tell you what we’ll do though,” said Brush to the would-be purchasers. “If you will give each one of us seventeen cakes, you can take the rabbit.”

The boys retired and held a private consultation, then came back, and the leader said, “We’ll take the rabbit.”

These boys must have coveted the rabbit very much, for there was not a boy in the school who did not love cake, and the one slice of brown ginger-cake we were each given for Sunday noon lunch was the only delicacy we tasted. This cake became a currency among the boys, and all contracts for cakes were faithfully kept. I know of only one instance where a boy failed to keep his bargain, and he was so persecuted by the other scholars that he was obliged to pay his debt in order to live in peace.

Brush thought he had put the price of the rabbit so high that it would not be accepted; but as we could not back out of our agreement, we were obliged to part with our ghost rabbit for eighty-five cakes.

As the number of the gang purchasing the rabbit was the same as ours, for seventeen Sundays these five boys went without their cakes, while each one of us enjoyed a double share.

===Chinese===
“不过我告诉你们这么办吧，”布拉什对这帮想买的说。“只要你们每人给我们十七块糕，兔子就归你们。”

男孩们退下去开了个小会，回来后领头的说：“我们买。”

这帮男孩准是太眼馋这兔子了，因为学校里没有男孩不爱糕的；我们每星期天午饭各分到的那一片红糖姜糕，是我们尝到的唯一美味。这糕在男孩们中间成了通货，所有糕的交易都严格遵守。我知道只有一回，一个男孩毁了约，他被其他学生排挤得没法子，为了过安生日子只好还了债。

布拉什原以为把兔价抬得这么高，对方必定不肯；可话已出口不能反悔，我们只好用我们的鬼兔子换了八十五块糕。

买兔子的那伙人跟我们人数一样多；于是连着十七个星期天，这五个男孩没糕吃，而我们每个人享用双份。

## XV Joe / 乔

===Original===
It was recess. The laughter and shouts of the boys, as they chased each other and wrestled, mingled with the song of the wren and other birds that inhabited the woods surrounding the school. Not less merry or boisterous were the laughter and calls of the girls, although their territory for play was limited and fenced in, to keep them from too free a communication with the rougher sex. Study and work were forgotten, and every boy and girl romped in the sunshine, and the atmosphere around seemed to be alive with happiness.

Suddenly the boys began to gather curiously around two objects upon the ground. The girls, seeing this unusual stir, came running to their fence, climbed up as far as they dared, and asked the nearest boys what it was that attracted so large a crowd.

It was a pitiful scene﻿—there, sitting on the green grass, was a crippled old woman of about seventy or eighty years, speaking in the kindest and gentlest of tones, with inflections of the voice hard to describe, but which brought to one’s mind the twittering of a mother bird to its young, and passing her crooked fingers and wrinkled hands over the brown back of a miserable, naked, little boy who was digging his chubby fists in his eyes to squeeze away the tears that flowed incessantly.

“Don’t cry! my little grandson,” she was saying; “don’t cry! These White-chests are kind; they will clothe and feed you. I can no longer take care of you, so I must give you to them. See these boys, what nice caps and coats and pants they have! You will have these things, too, and you will have plenty to eat. The White-chests will be good to you; I will come and see you very often. Don’t cry!”

===Chinese===
那是课间休息。男孩们互相追逐、扭打，笑声喊声与学校四周树林里鹪鹩和其他鸟儿的歌唱交织在一起。女孩们的欢笑呼喊也不见得少些、静些，虽然她们的游玩地盘有限，还用栅栏围了起来，免得她们同粗野的异性过于自由地往来。功课和活计都被忘到脑后，男孩女孩个个在太阳底下撒欢，四周的空气仿佛都活了，满是快活。

忽然，男孩们开始好奇地围向地上的两个身影。女孩们见这异常的骚动，跑到栅栏边，爬到不敢再爬的高度，问最近的男孩，是什么引来了这么大一群人。

那是一幅凄惨的景象——青草地上坐着一个七十来岁的瘸腿老妇，用最慈爱最温和的声调说着话，那语气的抑扬难以描摹，却叫人想起母鸟对雏鸟的啁啾；她弯曲的手指、起皱的手，摩挲着一个可怜的、光着身子的小男孩的棕色脊背，那孩子正把胖乎乎的拳头往眼睛里塞，想把流个不停的眼泪挤回去。

“别哭！我的小孙子，”她正说着；“别哭！这些白胸人心肠好；他们会给你穿衣、给你饭吃。我照看不动你了，只好把你交给他们。瞧这些男孩，他们的帽子、上衣和裤子多好看！你也会有的，还会有好多吃的。白胸人会待你好；我会常来看你。别哭！”

===Original===
But the boy cried all the harder, twisting his fists into his eyes, and the old woman continued her caressing twittering.

The bell rang, and there was a rush for the schoolroom. When the hard breathing, coughing, and shuffling into position at the desks had ceased, the door was gently pushed open, and the old woman entered, tenderly urging the unwilling little brown body forward into the room, still weeping. Addressing Graybeard, who was watching the scene with a queer smile on his lips, the old woman said:

“I have brought my little boy to give him to the White-chests to raise and to educate. On account of my age and feebleness, I am no longer able to care for him. I give him to you, and I beg that he be kindly treated. That is all I ask.”

Without waiting for an answer, the poor creature, with tears streaming down her furrowed cheeks, limped out of the room, making a cheerless clatter with her heavy stick as she moved away. The little boy, recovering from his bewilderment, turned to see if his grandmother was still near by, and, finding that she had gone, gave a piteous wail, and fell to the floor, sobbing violently.

===Chinese===
可男孩哭得更凶了，拳头直往眼睛里拧，老妇人继续她爱抚的啁啾。

铃响了，大家一窝蜂跑进教室。等喘气声、咳嗽声、各归各位的挪动声平息下来，门被轻轻推开，老妇人走进来，温存地把那不肯挪步的棕色小身子往前送，孩子还在哭。老妇人向嘴角挂着一丝古怪笑意的灰胡子开口道：

“我把我的小男孩带来了，把他交给白胸人抚养、受教育。我年纪大、身子弱，照看不动他了。我把孩子交给你，求你好好待他。我只求这一件事。”

不等回答，这可怜的人便淌下泪来，泪水顺着满是沟壑的脸颊流，拖着瘸腿出了屋，走开时沉重的拄杖敲出一串凄凉的声响。小男孩从怔忡中回过神来，扭头看祖母还在不在近旁；见她已经走了，发出一声哀号，扑倒在地，剧烈地抽泣起来。

===Original===
Who was this wretched little boy? He was his mother’s son, that’s all. He had no father, that is, none to caress and fondle him as other boys had. A man had presented the name of the boy to the Agent to be entered on the annuity rolls, only to that selfish extent recognizing the lad as his son.

The mother died while the child still needed her tender care, and the little one was left all alone in this great world that plays with the fortunes of men and nations. The place of death was in a dreary little tent, the rags of which flapped and fluttered in the force of the merciless winds, as though in sympathy with the melancholy situation. No loving husband or father was there to prepare the body for its last resting-place, and to give the helpless babe the nourishment for which it cried. Not even a relative was there; the dead woman had none among the people; she belonged to another tribe.

As the mother lay an unburied corpse, and her child wailing, a figure bent with age was plodding by. It was an old woman; slowly she put her heavy stick forward, then took a step, as though measuring every movement. When she came near the tent, she stopped, for the distressing wail had pierced her ears. She raised her trembling hand to her brow, looked up to the tent, then to the surroundings. The wailing went on, and the decrepit old woman hastened toward the tent as fast as she was able to go, and entered. For a moment she stood still, contemplating the scene before her, then from the fountains of her tender heart arose tears, impelled not by the sympathy that naturally springs from the love of friend or kindred, but by that nobler and higher feeling which lifts one toward God﻿—the sympathy for humankind.

Thus it was that this kindhearted old woman took the homeless little child to her tent and cared for him. The two were inseparable until the grandmother, as she was called by the boy, felt that she was fast approaching the time when she would be summoned to join her fathers in the spirit-land; so, to provide for the child’s future, she had brought him to the school.

===Chinese===
这个可怜的小男孩是谁？他是他母亲的儿子，仅此而已。他没有父亲——就是说，没有一个能像别的男孩的父亲那样疼他爱他的人。有个男人向代管官报过这孩子的名字，登上了年金名册——他对这儿子，也就只认到这份自私的地步。

母亲死时，孩子还离不得她的悉心照看；小家伙被孤零零撇在这个拨弄着人事和国运的大世界里。死的地方是一顶凄凉的小帐篷，帐篷的破布条在无情的风里噼啪乱飘，像是在应和这凄凉的处境。没有深情的丈夫或父亲在场为遗体料理后事，也没人给这无助的婴儿喂他啼哭着要的奶。连一个亲戚也没有；死去的女人在这族中无一亲人；她是外族的人。

母亲的尸身未葬，孩子啼哭，一个佝偻的身影蹒跚走过。是位老妇人；她慢慢把沉重的拄杖探向前，再挪一步，像是在掂量每一个动作。走近帐篷时她停住了，因为那凄惨的啼哭刺进了她的耳朵。她把颤抖的手举到额前，望了望帐篷，又望了望四周。哭声不停，衰朽的老妇人便使出她还能有的最快脚步朝帐篷赶去，进了帐篷。她静立了片刻，注视眼前的景象，随即从那颗柔软的心底涌出泪来——这泪不是由亲友之爱自然生出的同情催动的，而是那种更高尚、把人举向上帝的感情催动的——对人类同胞的同情。

就这样，这位好心的老妇人把这个无家可归的小娃娃带回自己的帐篷，抚养起来。祖孙俩形影不离，直到被孩子唤作祖母的她觉得自己被召去灵界与先人相聚的日子近了；于是，为孩子的将来打算，她把他送进了学校。

===Original===
The naming of a new pupil was usually an occasion for much merriment, but this time there was no enthusiasm. The school seemed to be in sympathy with the grandmother who went away weeping. Instead of raising their hands, as was their wont, to suggest names, they sought to hide their feeling by poring over their books.

“Come,” said Graybeard, “we must have a name for this youngster. Be quick and suggest one.”

There was no response. Finally a big boy, who was busy over his lessons, said without lifting his head, “Call him Joseph.”

So Graybeard entered that name on the school Register.

Joe, as he was called by the boys, grew rapidly, but the helplessness of infancy clung to him. Because he could not fight, he became the butt of every trick a schoolboy could devise, and there was no one who would do battle for him. If a big boy looked hard at him he would howl, and if one of his size rushed at him threateningly, he would shrink with fear. He was incapable of creating any mischief, yet he was continually stumbling into scrapes.

===Chinese===
给新学生起名通常是件叫大家乐不可支的事，可这一回谁也提不起兴致。全校仿佛都同情着那位哭着离去的老祖母。孩子们没有照老习惯举手提名，而是埋头盯住书本，想把心头的情绪掩过去。

“来吧，”灰胡子说，“总得给这小家伙起个名字。快点，提一个。”

没人应声。末了，一个正埋头功课的大男孩头也不抬地说：“就叫约瑟夫（Joseph）吧。”

于是灰胡子把这个名字登上了学校的学籍簿。

男孩们管他叫乔（Joe）。乔长得快，可幼时的孱弱一直缠着他。因为他不会打架，就成了学校男孩们想得出的每一种恶作剧的靶子，也没有谁肯为他出头。大男孩狠狠瞪他一眼，他就嚎；同岁数的冲他一比划，他就吓得直缩。他自己不会闯祸，却不断栽进祸里。

===Original===
One sultry afternoon as I was sitting in the shade of the walnut tree in front of the school, busy making a sling for Bob out of an old shoe, Joe came up to us, and dropped on his hands and knees. With the greatest interest he watched me cut the leather into a diamond shape; after a while he ventured to ask, “What yer makin’?”

“Wait and see,” I answered, and went on working. When I had finished the sling-strap or pocket, I cut from the lappets of my buckskin moccasin two strings, making a noose at the end of one, and then fastened both strings to the sling-strap. Although I did not say anything about it, I had determined to make one for Joe as soon as I had shown Bob how to use the sling. He tried to find out from Bob what I was making; but that little chap would not speak to him.

When the sling was finished, I told Bob to gather some rusty nails and pebbles. He was off with a jump, and returned with a good supply in an amazingly short time. Joe still sat watching, with eyes and mouth wide open. I put a nail in the sling-strap, and, to show Bob how to use the sling, swung it around three or four times, then threw out my arm with force, letting one end of the string slip, and the nail sped on its way through the air, singing. Bob clapped his hands with delight.

A crow was flying lazily over head, croaking as he went. I sent a stone whizzing up to him; it barely missed his head, and he turned a complete somersault in the air, to our great amusement.

“I’m goin’ to make one too!” said Joe, suddenly rising and hunting around for materials.

===Chinese===
一个闷热的下午，我正坐在校门前的核桃树荫里，忙着用一只旧鞋给鲍勃做弹弓，乔朝我们这边过来，手膝着地趴下。他兴致十足地看我把皮子剪成菱形；过了一会儿他壮着胆问：“你做啥呢？”

“等着瞧，”我答道，接着干活。弹弓兜做好后，我从鹿皮鞋的鞋耳上剪下两条皮绳，一条头上做个套环，再把两条绳都拴到弹弓兜上。虽然我嘴上没说，心里已经打定主意，等教会鲍勃用弹弓，就给乔也做一个。他缠着鲍勃打听我在做什么；可那小家伙不肯理他。

弹弓做好了，我叫鲍勃去捡些生锈的钉子和石子。他一蹦而起，没多大工夫就捡回来一大把。乔还坐在那儿，眼睛嘴巴张得老大。我把一颗钉子放进弹弓兜，为了给鲍勃示范，抡了三四圈，然后猛地甩出胳膊，松开一端的绳，钉子呼啸着穿过空中飞去。鲍勃乐得直拍手。

一只乌鸦懒洋洋地从头顶飞过，一路呀呀地叫。我把一颗石子嗖地朝它甩上去；差一点擦着它的头，它在空中翻了个完整的筋斗，把我们乐坏了。

“我也要做一个！”乔说着，猛地站起来，四下找材料。

===Original===
I paid no attention to him, but went on teaching Bob how to throw stones with the sling, little thinking that we were drifting toward an incident which gave Joe much pain temporarily and left an impression on my immature mind unfavorable to the White-chests which lasted many, many years.

“Mine’s done!” exclaimed Joe, holding up a sling he had made out of rotten rags.

“Don’t use it,” I made haste to say, “and I’ll make you a good one.”

He paid no heed to my words, but went on trying to balance stones in the old piece of rag. The stones dropped before he could swing the sling and throw them. Bob kept me busy throwing stones for him, for he was afraid of hitting the boys who were on the hillside near by playing tag, or of sending a pebble over the fence, where the girls were singing and chatting over some of their games.

“Look now, look!” cried Joe. I turned to see what he was doing. He had succeeded in balancing a clod of earth nearly as large as his head in the rag sling, and was about to swing it.

===Chinese===
我没理会他，继续教鲍勃用弹弓扔石头，万万没想到我们正滑向一场事故——它让乔一时吃了大苦头，也在我未定形的心里留下对白胸人的坏印象，许多许多年都消不掉。

“我的做好啦！”乔喊道，举起一把用烂布条做的弹弓。

“别用那个，”我赶紧说，“我给你做个好的。”

他把我的话当耳旁风，继续试着在破布片上放稳石子。还没等他抡起来扔，石子就掉了。鲍勃不停让我替他扔，因为山坡近旁有男孩在玩捉人，他怕砸着他们，也怕把石子甩过栅栏——女孩们正在那头唱着、聊着她们的游戏呢。

“快看，快看！”乔喊。我转身看他干什么。他居然把一块几乎有他脑袋大的土坷垃在破布弹弓上放稳了，正要抡起来。

===Original===
Just at this moment Graybeard came out of the carpenter’s shop and, shading his eyes with a newspaper, he called loudly to one of the boys who was playing tag, “Ulysees! Ulysees!” He inflated his lungs to call for the third time, and with greater volume of voice. Joe had swung the clod of earth around for the second time, and it was halfway up for the third round when the string broke; released from its holdings, the clod flew into the air, revolving, and dropping loose particles as it went. I held my breath as I watched it, for I saw just where it was going to strike.

In throwing a stone at some object, I used to imagine that by keeping a steady eye on the stone and bending my body in the direction I desired it to go, I could make the missile reach the place aimed at. In this instance, although I did not throw the clod, I unconsciously bent my body sidewise, keeping my eyes steadily on the lump of earth to draw it away from the spot for which it was making. The two other boys watched with frightened faces.

Graybeard, with head thrown back, lips parted, and chest expanded, called, “Uly﻿—!” when the diminutive planet, which I was trying to guide by my force of will, struck him in the chest, and burst in a thousand bits. For a moment there was coughing and sputtering; then Graybeard drew out his handkerchief, dusted his beard, and his white shirt front. He looked around to see where the missile that struck him came from. I wished that we three could sink into the earth, or else turn into nothing, as Graybeard’s eyes rested upon us.

“Come here!” he demanded with a vigorous gesture. Like so many guilty curs we walked up to him.

===Chinese===
就在这当口，灰胡子从木工房出来，用一张报纸遮着眼，大声喊一个玩捉人的男孩：“尤利西斯（Ulysees）！尤利西斯！”他鼓足肺叶要喊第三声，嗓门更大。乔已把那土坷垃抡了两圈，第三圈抡到半途，布条断了；土块脱了羁绊，打着转飞上天，一路撒着碎屑。我屏住呼吸望着它，因为我看清了它要落在哪儿。

朝什么东西扔石头时，我总爱想象：只要眼睛盯牢石头、把身子朝想要它去的方向偏过去，就能让这飞弹命中目标。这一回，土块虽不是我扔的，我却不自觉地侧过身子，眼睛死死盯住那块土，想凭意念把它从它要去的那个点上引开。另外两个男孩脸色发白地看着。

灰胡子头向后仰，张着嘴，挺着胸，喊出“尤利——！”就在这时，这颗我正试图用意志力驾驭的小小行星撞在他胸口，炸成千百块。一时间他连呛带咳；随后灰胡子掏出手帕，掸掸胡子，掸掸白衬衫前襟。他四下张望，看那击中他的飞弹来自何方。灰胡子的目光落到我们身上时，我真希望我们仨能钻进地里，或者干脆化为乌有。

“过来！”他猛一挥手势喝道。我们像一群做了亏心事的小狗，蹭到他跟前。

===Original===
“Which one of you did it?” he cried, grasping me by the collar and shaking me until my teeth chattered.

Joe cringed and cried; it was a confession. I was about to say, “he didn’t mean to do it;” when the infuriated man turned, went into the shop, and in a moment came back with a piece of board.

“Hold out your hand!” he said, addressing the shrinking boy.

Joe timidly held out his left hand, keeping his eyes all the while on the uplifted board, which came down with force, but not on the little hand that had been withdrawn to escape the blow. Graybeard sprang at the boy, caught his hand, and attempted to strike it; but the boy pulled away and the board fell with a vicious thud on the wrist of the man, who now turned white with rage. Catching a firm grip on the hand of the boy, Graybeard dealt blow after blow on the visibly swelling hand. The man seemed to lose all self-control, gritting his teeth and breathing heavily, while the child writhed with pain, turned blue, and lost his breath.

===Chinese===
“你们谁干的？”他一把揪住我的领子吼道，抖得我牙齿打颤。

乔缩着哭；这等于招认了。我正想说“他不是故意的”，那狂怒的人转身进了木工房，转眼拿了块木板回来。

“把手伸出来！”他对那缩成一团的男孩说。

乔怯生生伸出左手，眼睛始终盯着那高高举起的木板；板子狠狠落下，却没落在那只缩回去躲这一击的小手上。灰胡子扑向男孩，抓住他的手又要打；可男孩一挣，木板狠狠一声闷响落在汉子的手腕上，他气得脸都白了。灰胡子死死攥住男孩的手，一板接一板落在那只眼看着肿起来的手上。那人似乎全然失了自持，咬着牙，喘着粗气，孩子痛得直扭，脸都青了，气也接不上来。

===Original===
It was a horrible sight. The scene in the schoolroom when the naked little boy was first brought there by the old woman rose before me; I heard the words of the grandmother as she gave the boy to Graybeard, “I beg that he be kindly treated; that is all I ask!” And she had told the child that the White-chests would be kind to him.

Poor Joe, I did what I never would have done if a boy of his own size had thrashed him, I took him by the hand and tried to comfort him, and cared for his bruises.

As for Graybeard, I did not care in the least about the violent shaking he had given me; but the vengeful way in which he fell upon that innocent boy created in my heart a hatred that was hard to conquer.

The day was spoiled for me; I partly blamed myself for it, though my plans had been to make the two little boys happy, but misery came instead. After supper I slipped away from my companions, and all alone I lay on the grass looking up at the stars, thinking of what had happened that afternoon. I tried to reconcile the act of Graybeard with the teachings of the Missionaries, but I could not do so from any point of view.

All the boys had come together in the yard, and someone called out, “Let’s play pull.” So they divided into two groups, grading each according to the size of the boys. Two of the strongest were selected, one from each side; they held a stout stick between them, then on each side the boys grasped each other around the waist. When all were ready, they began to pull, every boy crying, “Hue! Hue!” as he tugged and strained. In the dusk the contending lines looked like two great dark beasts tearing at each other and lashing their tails from side to side. Bob and Joe were at the very end of one side; Bob had tied a bit of rope around his waist, and Joe had hold of that with his only serviceable hand. The pulling lasted for quite a while; finally one side drew the other over the mark; the game ended, and the boys noisily disbanded.

===Chinese===
那情景可怕极了。老妇人把光身的小男孩初次带进教室的那一幕浮上眼前；我听见老祖母把孩子交给灰胡子时说的话：“求你好好待他；我只求这一件事！”她可是告诉过孩子，白胸人会待他好的。

可怜的乔——我做了件换作同龄男孩揍他一顿我也绝不会做的事：我拉起他的手，想法子安慰他，照料他的伤。

至于灰胡子，他把我狠命抖那几下，我毫不在意；可他扑向那个无辜孩子的那种凶狠报复的样子，在我心里激起一股久久压不下去的恨。

这一天算让我给毁了；我多少怪自己，虽然我的本意是让两个小男孩快活，来的却是苦痛。晚饭后我悄悄离开同伴，独自躺在草地上望着星星，想那天下午的事。我想把灰胡子的行为同传教士们的教诲调和起来，可无论从哪个角度看都调和不了。

男孩们都聚到院子里，有人喊：“玩拔河吧。”于是分成两拨，按男孩个头大小搭配。两边各选一个最壮的；两人中间攥住一根粗棍，然后每边的男孩彼此搂住腰。一切就绪便开始拔，每个男孩一边使劲一边喊：“嘿！嘿！”暮色里，对峙的两条长队像两头互相撕咬的大黑兽，尾巴左右直甩。鲍勃和乔在一边的末尾；鲍勃腰上拴了截绳子，乔用他那只还能使的手攥着。拔了好一阵；最后一边把另一边拉过了界；游戏结束，男孩们闹哄哄地散了。

===Original===
“Frank! Frank!” I heard; it was Edwin and the rest of the gang.

“Here I am,” I called out, and they gathered around me.

“Joe’s hand is awful swelled up,” said Bob, as he threw himself down on the grass.

“What’s the matter with him?” asked Warren.

“Graybeard beat Joe’s hand like everything; he was so mad I thought he’d kill the boy.” Then I recounted the scene, adding, “I can’t think of anything else; it was awful!”

===Chinese===
“弗兰克！弗兰克！”我听见有人喊；是埃德温和那伙人。

“我在这儿，”我应了一声，他们围拢过来。

“乔的手肿得吓人，”鲍勃说着，一屁股躺倒在草地上。

“他怎么了？”沃伦问。

“灰胡子把乔的手往死里打；他气成那样，我都当他要把孩子打死了。”接着我把那一幕讲了一遍，又说：“我别的什么也想不了；太吓人了！”

===Original===
“Did he do anything to you?” asked Edwin.

“He shook me right hard when he asked me who did it; but when he saw Joe crying he knew who it was; then he let go of me and whipped him.”

Brush had been listening to my story without a word; now he arose and said, “Boys, stay here till I come back.”

He went into the house and knocked at the superintendent’s door.

===Chinese===
“他对你动手了吗？”埃德温问。

“他问是谁干的，把我好一顿抖；可他见乔哭了，就知道是谁了；就放开我去打他了。”

布拉什一直一声不吭地听我讲；这时他站起来说：“伙计们，在这儿等我回来。”

他进了屋，敲校监的门。

===Original===
“I’m glad to see you Brush,” said the superintendent, kindly. “Have you finished the book, and do you want another?”

“No, sir; I wish to speak to you about something that happened today, which I don’t think is quite right, and I thought you ought to know about it.” Then he told in a simple straightforward manner the story of Joe’s punishment.

When Brush had finished, the superintendent sent for Graybeard. For a long time the two men talked earnestly together. At length Brush returned, and said, as he took his seat among us:

“Boys, that will not happen again. Graybeard says he’s sorry he did it, and I believe him.”

===Chinese===
“很高兴见到你，布拉什，”校监和气地说。“书读完了吗？还要一本吗？”

“不，先生；我想跟您说说今天发生的一件事，我觉得这事不太对，想来您应该知道。”于是他朴直地把乔挨罚的事讲了一遍。

布拉什讲完，校监派人叫来灰胡子。两个男人恳切地谈了许久。末了布拉什回来，在我们中间坐下，说：

“伙计们，这种事不会再发生了。灰胡子说他后悔那么做，我相信他。”

## VI Lester / 莱斯特

===Original===
The hands of the little clock on Graybeard’s desk indicated the hour of two. The midsummer’s sun hurled its rays with unrelenting force to the earth, and the wind, as though consenting to the attack, withheld its refreshing breezes. All the windows of our schoolroom were thrown wide open, and the hum of busy insects and the occasional cry of a bird were the only sounds that relieved the monotonous stillness outside.

A class, with Warren at the head, was on the floor. The girl at the foot was reading in a tone that made it difficult to resist the drowsiness that attacked everyone in the room. She came to a hard word, and, according to our custom, she spelled it. Graybeard, who was sitting with eyes shut, pronounced it for her through a suppressed yawn. A few more words brought her to the end of the paragraph.

A long pause followed; Warren stood with book uplifted, but was gazing intently on something outside. The teacher, recovering from an overbalancing nod, opened his eyes slowly, and lazily called, “Warren!” The boy did not stir. Brush and I looked up from our desk, and shuffled our feet to attract his attention. “Warren!” again called Graybeard, in a louder tone. Still there was no response.

Brush tore a flyleaf out of his book, rolled it hastily into a ball, and threw it at Warren’s head, but missed it.

===Chinese===
灰胡子桌上小钟的指针指着两点。盛夏的太阳把光线毫不留情地砸向大地，风也像是在同谋这场进攻，收起了凉爽的气息。我们教室所有的窗户都敞得大开，忙碌的昆虫的嗡嗡声和偶尔的一声鸟啼，是唯一给外面单调的寂静解闷的声响。

沃伦领头的那个班正站在台前。队尾的女孩用一种让满屋人都难挡睡意的调子读着课文。她碰到一个难词，按我们的规矩把它拼读了一遍。闭着眼睛坐着的灰胡子忍着呵欠替她念了出来。又读了几个词，她读到了段落末尾。

接着是长长的停顿；沃伦举着书站着，眼睛却直勾勾盯着外面的什么东西。老师从一次失衡的点头里缓过神来，慢慢睁开眼，懒洋洋地喊：“沃伦！”男孩一动不动。布拉什和我从课桌上抬起头，蹭着脚想引起他注意。“沃伦！”灰胡子又喊，嗓门高了些。还是没有回应。

布拉什从书上撕下一页衬页，匆忙揉成团，朝沃伦的脑袋扔去，没打中。

===Original===
Graybeard turned in his chair, his eyes rested upon the boy, who was still looking fixedly out of the window. Then he rose, stepped softly up to Warren, seized him by the shoulders and shook him violently, saying, “Are you asleep?”

“Swarming!” rang out the last word of the sentence which Warren was making a desperate effort to utter.

Graybeard, following the eyes of the lad, looked out of the window, “Quick, boys, to the dining-room, take anything you can make a noise with!” he exclaimed, as he sprang to the door, threw it open with a bang and disappeared.

We leaped over desks, and tumbled over each other as we rushed with impetuous haste to the dining-room. Brush caught up an enormous tin pan, Edwin a milk pail, and I the school triangle; the rest of the boys took tin pans and plates, or whatever they could lay their hands on, and we all ran out into the yard. Warren was already following the humming black cloud, ringing the school-bell with all his might. We caught up with him, and began beating on the tin pans with our knuckles, keeping up a constant yelling like a lot of savages. The noise we made was enough to drive the bees and ourselves insane. It was bedlam let loose. On we went through the barnyard, up the hill, and into the woods, closely following the flying black mass. Three boys carrying small mirrors kept throwing flashes of light into the swarm.

===Chinese===
灰胡子在椅子里转过身，目光落在那还在呆望窗外的男孩身上。他随即起身，轻步走到沃伦跟前，一把攥住他的肩膀狠狠摇晃，说：“你睡着了？”

“分群！”沃伦憋足了劲要说出的那句话，最后这个词脱口而出。

灰胡子顺着男孩的目光望向窗外，“快，孩子们，去饭厅，能弄响的家伙什么都拿上！”他喊着一跃而起，砰地推开门，人已经不见了。

我们跨过课桌，跌跌撞撞、争先恐后地冲进饭厅。布拉什抓起一只大得吓人的锡盆，埃德温拎了一只奶桶，我拿了学校的报时铁板；其余男孩拿锡盆锡盘，逮着什么拿什么，我们全都跑进院子。沃伦已经跟着那片嗡嗡的黑云去了，正拼命摇着校铃。我们赶上他，开始用指节敲锡盆，不住地嚎叫，活像一群野人。我们闹出的动静足以把蜜蜂和我们自己统统逼疯。简直是打开了疯人院。我们一路穿过牲口院、上山、钻进树林，紧追着那团飞动的黑云。三个男孩拿着小镜子，不停朝蜂群晃出闪光。

===Original===
The bees made a straight line for a tall oak, hovered over the end of a high branch, and then settled on it. We gathered around the tree, and continued our unearthly noise until Graybeard, with a box and a saw on his shoulder, and a coil of rope on his arm, came up puffing and all in a perspiration.

“Have they settled?” he asked, shading his eyes and looking up into the tree.

“Yes, there they are,” answered Brush, pointing to the writhing black mass on the branch.

“Who can climb?” said Graybeard, looking around among the boys. No one answered. After a while Edwin spoke up, “Lester climb tree like wildcat.”

===Chinese===
蜜蜂笔直朝一棵高大的橡树飞去，在一根高枝末端上方盘旋了一阵，随即落定。我们围到树下，继续制造那不像人间的噪音，直到灰胡子肩上扛着一只箱子和一把锯、胳膊上挎着一圈绳，气喘吁吁、大汗淋漓地赶来。

“落定了没有？”他手搭凉棚、仰望着树上问。

“落定了，在那儿呢，”布拉什指着树枝上那团蠕动的黑团答道。

“谁会爬树？”灰胡子环视男孩们。没人应声。过了一会儿埃德温开了口：“莱斯特爬树像野猫。”

===Original===
Lester turned and looked daggers at him. Brush and I nudged each other and giggled. Edwin was playing a joke on Lester.

“Come,” said Graybeard, “there’s no time to be lost.” And he proceeded to tie the end of the rope around the waist of Lester, who had not recovered from his astonishment and was given no time to put in a disclaimer to the title of climber.

Graybeard lifted the lad up as high as he could, then the boy began to climb. He went up slowly but surely, dragging the rope after him. Edwin shouted words of encouragement. “That’s good, go ahead!” he would exclaim as the climber made now and again six inches or so.

“Wait till I get down, I show you!” Lester called back. Then Edwin turned to us and grinned.

The limb upon which the bees had settled was at last reached; the boy pulled up the handsaw that was tied to the other end of the rope. He looked down at us with mischief in his face, then straddled the branch with his face toward the trunk of the tree and began to saw. Graybeard, seeing this, called up in great excitement, “Stop! stop! Lester, stop! Turn the other way.” The boy, having had his fun, turned, and, moving as near to the bees as he dared, began sawing slowly until the branch hung down, then he severed it. It did not fall because before he began to saw he had tied one end of the rope near to the bees, and had fastened the other part near to the place where he was sitting, so that he was able gradually to lower the bees to the ground.

===Chinese===
莱斯特回头恶狠狠瞪了他一眼。布拉什和我互相捅了捅，咯咯直乐。埃德温在捉弄莱斯特。

“来吧，”灰胡子说，“一刻也耽误不得。”说着他把绳子一头拴到莱斯特腰上；莱斯特惊魂未定，连申辩自己不会爬树的机会都没有。

灰胡子把少年高高托起，男孩开始往上爬。他爬得慢却稳，身后拖着绳子。埃德温高喊着给他打气。“好样的，接着爬！”每当爬树的人又挪上六英寸左右，他就喊一声。

“等我下来瞧我怎么收拾你！”莱斯特回头喊。埃德温转身朝我们咧嘴一笑。

蜜蜂落定的那根枝子终于到了；男孩把拴在绳子另一头的手锯拉了上去。他朝我们俯视，一脸坏笑，然后跨骑上树枝，脸朝着树干，开始锯起来。灰胡子一见，激动得大声朝上喊：“停！停！莱斯特，停！掉头朝那边。”男孩闹够了，转过身，壮着胆子挪到离蜜蜂最近的地方，慢慢锯着，直到树枝垂下来，然后把它锯断了。树枝没有掉下去，因为动手锯之前，他已把绳子一端拴在蜜蜂近旁，另一端拴在自己坐处附近，这样就能把蜜蜂慢慢放到地上。

===Original===
We did not know that anything had happened to Lester until he came down, then we saw that he was stung on the eyebrow and his face was swollen. Brush moistened a bit of earth and smeared it around the injured part to prevent further swelling, but it did no good.

Graybeard put the box over the bees and began pounding the top, “Look under there, Frank, and see if they are going up,” he said; “if the queen goes, they will all go.”

I crouched to the ground and looked into the box; there was great activity and noise. “I think they are going up,” I said.

Suddenly the pounding on the box ceased; I heard an outcry and a groan; I looked up, and there was Graybeard rolling on the ground. He was badly stung in the face. Brush went to his assistance and painted his wounds with mud. I went to the box and pounded as Graybeard had done.

“Look under, Warren, and see what they are doing,” I said.

===Chinese===
直到莱斯特下来，我们才知道他出了事：眉毛上挨了蜇，脸都肿了。布拉什和了一撮泥，抹在伤处四周想止住再肿，却不管用。

灰胡子把箱子扣在蜜蜂上，敲起箱顶来。“弗兰克，往底下看，看它们进不进去，”他说；“蜂王进去，它们就全进去了。”

我蹲到地上朝箱里看；里面一派忙碌和喧闹。“我看它们在往里进。”我说。

突然，敲箱声停了；我听见一声惨叫一声呻吟；抬头一看，灰胡子正在地上打滚。他脸上挨了狠蜇。布拉什赶去救他，给他伤口涂上泥。我走到箱子跟前，学着灰胡子的样子敲。

“沃伦，往底下看，看它们在干什么。”我说。

===Original===
Warren put his head to the ground and looked, “I guess that old king went up; they’re all gone,” he said; “I can’t see them.”

Having recaptured our bees, we securely fastened the box so that the wind could not blow it over; we gathered up our pans, milk pails, and bells and formed a homeward procession. Brush headed it, leading Graybeard, whose eyes were now both closed and bandaged with his white handkerchief, and in this way we reached the Mission building.

The ladies and the school girls were waiting on the porch for our return, and as we approached the gate a number called out, “How many of you are stung?”

“Two!” cried the boys; “teacher and Lester.”

When we were passing the girls on the porch to go to our quarters, pretty little black-eyed Rosalie, my sweetheart, came up to me and asked, “Frank, was you stung?”

“No; but the bees wouldn’t go in the box for anybody but me,” I answered proudly.

===Chinese===
沃伦把头贴到地上看。“我看那只老蜂王进去了；全进去了，”他说；“我看不见它们了。”

收回了我们的蜜蜂，我们把箱子拴牢，风吹不翻；收起锡盆、奶桶和铃，排成一支回家的队伍。布拉什领头，牵着灰胡子——他两眼都睁不开了，用白手帕缠着——就这样我们回到了传教所的楼前。

女士们和女学生们都在廊子上等我们回来；我们走近大门时，好几个声音喊道：“你们谁挨蜇了？”

“两个！”男孩们喊；“老师和莱斯特。”

我们从廊子上的女孩们面前走过、往宿舍去的时候，我那黑眼睛的漂亮小心肝罗莎莉迎上来问我：“弗兰克，你挨蜇了吗？”

“没有；可除了我，蜜蜂谁的话也不听，肯进箱子。”我骄傲地答道。

===Original===
“But I wish you was stung like Lester,” she said; “his girl is telling the rest of them all about it, and they think he’s right smart because he got stung.”

Some of the big girls, overhearing this confidence, put their aprons up to their faces to hide their laughter. The teachers never knew that there were lovers among the pupils and that little romances were going on right under their eyes.

Graybeard could not see us to bed that night, so the superintendent took his place.

“Good night, boys, keep quiet and go to sleep,” he said as he went downstairs after he had heard us say our prayer.

“Warren, you’ve earned ten cents today,” said Brush; “I think Lester earned something too. I don’t know how much it’s going to be, but I’ll go and see the superintendent about it tomorrow.”

“Say, Brush, I think that bee that stung Lester was a drone; that’s why his face is all swelled up,” I said.

===Chinese===
“可我倒希望你像莱斯特那样挨蜇，”她说；“他的姑娘正在跟她们大讲特讲呢，她们都因为他挨了蜇，觉得他特英雄。”

几个大女孩偷听到了这番体己话，把围裙掩到脸上藏笑。教师们从不知道学生里头有情人，从不知道小儿女的情事就在他们眼皮底下进行。

那天晚上灰胡子看不见我们上床，校监便替了他。

“晚安，孩子们，安静睡觉，”他听我们念完祷文、下楼时说。

“沃伦，你今天挣了十美分，”布拉什说；“我看莱斯特也挣了点什么。不知道能有多少，明天我去问问校监。”

“哎，布拉什，我看蜇莱斯特的那只蜜蜂是只雄蜂；不然他的脸怎么肿成那样。”我说。

===Original===
“Oh! go ’long!” he answered. “Whoever heard of a drone having a sting. They have no sting, and they can’t sting. It’s only the working bees that have a sting.”

“But those drones are big fellows, two times as big as the working bees. The superintendent showed me one when he was moving a swarm to a new box in the bee house.”

“They haven’t any sting, though. There are three kinds of bees: there’s the queen, then there’s the drone, and there’s the working bees. When the drones get too many, and eat too much, the working bees, they get mad and they sting them to death.”

“I think that work bee thought Lester was drone,” remarked Edwin.

“Wait till I get well,” threatened Lester; “I’ll show you drone!”

“What is the queen?” asked Warren. “And what does it do?”

“Why a queen is a female king,” explained Brush, who was authority on a great many things. “She doesn’t do anything but sit on a big throne and tell people what to do. If they don’t mind her, she makes her soldiers cut their heads off. It’s the same with bees: they have a queen﻿—I don’t think she sits on a throne, but she tells the rest of the bees what to do; and if they don’t mind her, she gets up and goes; then all the rest have to follow her, because they won’t know what to do unless she tells them. That’s what that old queen did today.”

===Chinese===
“哦，去你的！”他答。“谁听说过雄蜂有刺。它们没有刺，蜇不了人。只有工蜂才有刺。”

“可雄蜂个头大，比工蜂大两倍。校监在蜂房里把一箱蜂挪到新箱里去的时候，指给我看过一只。”

“反正它们没有刺。蜜蜂有三种：有蜂王，有雄蜂，还有工蜂。雄蜂太多了、吃得太多了，工蜂就发了火，把它们活活蜇死。”

“我看那只工蜂把莱斯特当成雄蜂了。”埃德温评了一句。

“等我好了瞧，”莱斯特威吓道；“我让你们瞧瞧什么是雄蜂！”

“蜂王是什么？”沃伦问。“它是干什么的？”

“蜂王嘛，就是女身的王，”布拉什解释道，他在好多事情上都是权威。“她什么都不干，只坐在大宝座上，吩咐人做这做那。谁不听话，她就叫她的兵把他们的脑袋砍掉。蜜蜂也一样：它们有个蜂王——我看她不坐宝座，可她吩咐其余的蜂做事；要是它们不听，她就起身走掉；其余的就只好全跟着她走，因为她不说，它们就不知道该干什么。今天那只老蜂王就是这么干的。”

===Original===
“Why don’t the ’Mericans have a king?” asked Edwin. “They got a President, but I don’t think he’s big like a king.”

“They had one,” said Brush; “but they didn’t like him, because he put a terrible big tax on tea. The ’Mericans are awfully fond of tea, and when they saw they’d have to pay the trader and the king, too, for their tea, they got mad; and one night, when everybody was asleep, they painted up like wild Indians, and they got into a boat and paddled out to the tea ship and climbed in. They hollered and yelled like everything, and scared everybody; then they spilted the tea into the ocean.”

“What did the old king do?” asked Lester.

“Well, he was hopping mad, and he lifted his great big sceptre, and he went up to the man that brought the news, and knocked him over. Then he walked up and down talking loud, and when he got tired he went to his throne and sat down hard.”

“What is a sceptre?” I asked, interrupting the story.

===Chinese===
“美国人干吗不要国王？”埃德温问。“他们有个总统，可我看他不像国王那么大。”

“从前有过一个，”布拉什说；“可他们不喜欢他，因为他在茶上加了吓死人的重税。美国人爱茶爱得要命，一看买茶叶既要付钱给商人、又要孝敬国王，就火了；一天夜里，趁大家都睡着，他们把自己涂画成野印第安人，上了船，划到运茶的船边爬了进去。他们没命地又喊又叫，把人人都吓坏了；然后把茶叶全倒进了海里。”

“那老国王怎么办的？”莱斯特问。

“嗐，他暴跳如雷，举起他那根老大老大的权杖，走到报信的人跟前，一杖把他打翻。然后他来回踱步，大声嚷嚷，嚷累了，就走回宝座，一屁股坐下。”

“权杖是什么？”我插进故事问。

===Original===
“Why, it’s something like a war club; when the king tells people to do things, he shakes it at them, so they will get scared and mind what he says.”

“I wouldn’t mind him,” said Warren; “I’d make a big sceptre for myself and shake it at him.”

“Well,” continued Brush, “the old king sat still for a long time; then he said to his soldiers, you go and fight those ’Mericans. And they did fight, and had the Rev’lution. That war lasted eight years, and the king’s soldiers got licked. Then the ’Mericans made General George Washington their President because he couldn’t tell a lie.”

The next morning Brush went to the superintendent’s study, and soon came out calling for Warren and Lester. Edwin and I waited under the walnut-tree in front of the school. When the three came to us, they showed us a bright silver dime and an equally bright quarter of a dollar. According to our notions, Warren and his brother were rich, the former having earned the reward offered for the discovery and report of the swarming of the bees, and the latter earning the quarter by climbing the tree on which the swarm had settled.

Brush announced to us that Lester and Warren had been detailed to go after the mail. The post-office was in the trader’s store three miles away from the school, and boys were always very glad to be sent on this errand.

===Chinese===
“嗐，就是根跟打仗用的棍棒差不多的东西；国王吩咐人做事时，就朝他们摇晃它，好叫他们害怕，听他的话。”

“我就不听他的，”沃伦说；“我自己做根更大的权杖，冲他摇。”

“后来呀，”布拉什接着讲，“那老国王坐了好半天；然后对士兵们说，你们去跟那些美国人打。他们真打了，打出了那场大革命。那仗打了八年，国王的兵被打败了。于是美国人让乔治·华盛顿将军当总统，因为他不会说谎。”

第二天早晨，布拉什去了校监的书房，很快就出来喊沃伦和莱斯特。埃德温和我在校门前的核桃树下等。三人来到我们跟前，给我们看一枚亮闪闪的银质十美分硬币和一枚同样亮闪闪的两毛五银币。照我们的看法，沃伦和他哥哥发财了：前者挣到了发现并报告蜂群分飞的赏钱，后者靠爬上蜂群落定的那棵树，挣到了那枚两毛五。

布拉什向我们宣布，莱斯特和沃伦被派去取邮件了。邮局设在离学校三英里的商人铺子里，男孩们巴不得被派去跑这趟差。

===Original===
In the afternoon, when school was out, Brush went up to the superintendent’s room to borrow the spyglass, while Edwin and I went in search of Lester and Warren, who had slipped away from us. We could not find them, so we returned to the schoolroom, where we met Brush, and we all went up to the belfry.

The Indians were at work in their fields, and we each took the glass in turn to see if we could recognize our friends. Suddenly Edwin said, “Something’s going to happen; look at those girls.”

Two girls were going through the yard arm in arm, now and again glancing over their shoulders toward the boys’ playground. They reached the farthest corner of the yard, then turned and looked along the dividing fence. Two boys sauntered towards them on the other side, following a narrow path.

“There’s Lester and Warren,” said Brush; “they’re up to something, keep your eyes upon them.”

===Chinese===
下午放学后，布拉什上校监房间去借望远镜，埃德温和我去找莱斯特和沃伦——他俩溜得没了影。找不着，我们回到教室，在那儿碰上布拉什，于是一同上了钟楼。

印第安人正在地里干活，我们轮流用望远镜看能不能认出自己的朋友。忽然埃德温说：“要出事了；瞧那些女孩。”

两个姑娘挽着胳膊穿过场院，不时回头朝男孩的操场张望。她们走到场院最远的角落，转身顺着隔开的栅栏望去。两个男孩在栅栏那头，沿着一条窄径慢悠悠朝她们荡过去。

“是莱斯特和沃伦，”布拉什说；“他们有勾当，盯住他们。”

===Original===
We did. The four met at the corner, sat down and appeared to be talking to each other. When they had been there for some time, the boys handed through the palings to each of the girls a brown parcel.

“I see now why those boys wanted to go after the mail this morning,” said Brush.

The girls arose and walked toward the house, opening their parcels, and we saw through the spyglass that they were eating candy. The boys slowly returned, one following the other along the narrow path. Edwin thrust his fingers into his mouth and whistled, imitating the cry of the robin, which was the signal we five had adopted. The boys stopped suddenly as the sound reached them, and looked all around. Seeing no one, they went on. Again Edwin whistled; then I touched the bell very lightly with the clapper. The boys looked up to the belfry; but we kept out of sight.

At breakfast the next morning the two girls appeared at the table with their hair neatly done up in bright-colored ribbons. Edwin leaned over toward Lester and said in a whisper, “Your girl’s got a right pretty ribbon!”

“Yours hasn’t got any!” retorted Lester.

===Chinese===
我们盯着。四个人在角落会合，坐下，像是在交谈。待了一阵，男孩们从栅栏板缝里递给每个姑娘一个棕色的小包。

“我这下明白了，那两个家伙今早为什么抢着去取邮件。”布拉什说。

姑娘们站起身朝屋里走，一面拆小包；我们从望远镜里看见她们在吃糖。男孩们慢慢往回走，一前一后沿着那条窄径。埃德温把手指插进嘴里，吹了一声知更鸟叫——那是我们五个约定的暗号。声音传到，两个男孩猛地站住，四下张望。见没有人，接着走。埃德温又吹了一声；我拿钟锤极轻地碰了一下钟。男孩们抬头望向钟楼；可我们藏得严严实实。

第二天早饭时，那两个姑娘出现在桌旁，头发用颜色鲜亮的缎带整整齐齐扎好。埃德温俯身凑近莱斯特，小声说：“你的姑娘扎了条特漂亮的缎带！”

“你的什么也没扎！”莱斯特回敬道。

## List of Illustrations / 插图目录

===Original===
1. Sheet music for the Children’s Song, “Follow My Leader.”

===Chinese===
1. 儿童歌曲《跟着领队走》（Follow My Leader）的乐谱。

## IV Little Bob / 小鲍勃

===Original===
The afternoon session was over; Graybeard tapped his bell; we put away our books, folded our arms, and when there was silence the teacher spoke: “Frank will remain here until he finishes correctly the sum he is working on. He has neglected his arithmetic lesson during school hours, so he will have to do the work after school.”

Such punishment had not happened to me before. It had frequently come to other scholars, and I had felt sorry for them; but now the disgrace had fallen on me, and I felt it keenly.

Graybeard led the song about “The Little Brown Church in the Wild Wood,” and the whole school sang; but just then I did not care for brown churches or churches of any other color, so my voice did not mingle with that of the other pupils. Then they sang “Lord dismiss us,” but as I was not dismissed I did not join in the singing of that familiar hymn.

Brush, Edwin, and the rest of my companions lingered awhile in the schoolroom to keep me company; but as they had work to do they could not stay long, so I was left alone to struggle with a lot of ugly fractions. My thoughts ran in every direction, off to my home, to the boys at play, and anywhere but on my task. I made a desperate effort to bring myself around to the problem that held me a prisoner by keeping a steady gaze into the deep blue sky through the open window, and then slowly the solution of that detestable sum came to my mind, and I had it. I put it on my slate, compared it with the answer left me by Graybeard, found it correct, and my work was done.

===Chinese===
下午的课结束了；灰胡子敲了敲铃；我们收起书本，垂手抱臂坐好；静下来后，老师说：“弗兰克做完他手上的那道算术题、做对了才能走。上课时间他荒废了算术课，只好放学后补做。”

这样的惩罚从前没落到过我头上。别的学生常挨这一着，我一直替他们难过；如今这耻辱落到了我身上，我尝到了它的分量。

灰胡子起了个头，唱起那首《荒林中的棕色小教堂》，全校跟着唱；可那一刻我对棕色教堂也罢、随便什么颜色的教堂也罢都毫无兴致，所以我的声音没有混进学生们的歌声。随后他们唱《主啊，释我们回去》，可我并没有被释放，也就没参加那首熟得不能再熟的圣歌。

布拉什、埃德温和其余同伴在教室里陪我待了一阵；可他们都有活计，不能久留，于是我独自一人同一堆可恶的分数较劲。我的心思四处乱跑，跑回家去、跑到玩耍的男孩们那儿，就是不落在习题上。我拼命把自己拽回那道把我扣押起来的难题上——办法是透过敞开的窗户死死盯住深蓝的天空——渐渐地，那道可憎的算术题的解法浮上心头，我做出来了。我把它写上石板，跟灰胡子留给我的答案对了对，发现没错，功课完成了。

===Original===
I arose, put my books away, and stood near the teacher’s desk wondering what to do next, when all of a sudden the door burst open and in rushed a little boy, crying. He was without his hat, his coat unbuttoned, and shoestrings untied. Following swiftly on the little chap came a large boy who, for some reason, was angered at the fleeing lad, and was now pursuing to punish him. The little boy ran around the stove, then toward me and got behind me. The big boy pushed on in his vengeful pursuit, and reached to grasp the object of his anger when I struck at him with my fist. The blow fell on his forehead, he stood for a moment stunned; then he sprang at me; we dealt each other blow after blow, and in our mad charges we knocked over benches and desks. How it happened I do not know, for in my excitement I could not tell where I struck him, or where he struck me, but suddenly my antagonist put his hands to his stomach, doubled over and could not breathe. I became frightened. At length, with a succession of hiccups, the boy recovered his breath, picked up his hat, and went out.

I straightened out the benches and desks that we had knocked over, and then sat down to cool off. When I had rested, I called to the round-headed little chap who stood trembling in the corner holding up his trousers, for in his attempts to escape he had lost the buttons to his pants, “What did you do to that boy; what did he want to hit you for?”

“I didn’t do nothin’,” he answered, hitching up his garments as he came toward me.

“What’s your name?”

===Chinese===
我站起身，收拾好书本，站在讲台旁琢磨下一步做什么，忽然门砰地推开，一个小男孩哭着冲了进来。他没戴帽子，上衣敞着，鞋带也没系。紧跟着那小家伙追进来一个大男孩，不知为什么正对这逃跑的孩子光火，一路追来要教训他。小男孩绕着炉子跑，又朝我跑来，躲到我身后。大男孩挟着报复的怒气逼近，伸手要抓他泄愤的对象，我一拳朝他打去。这一拳落在他额头上，他愣了片刻；随即朝我扑来；我们你来我往，一拳接一拳，疯狂冲撞中把长凳和课桌都掀翻了。怎么收的场我不清楚，因为打昏了头，记不得打中了他哪儿、他又打中了我哪儿；忽然我的对手双手捂住肚子，弯下腰喘不上气来。我吓坏了。好半天，那男孩连打了一串嗝，气缓过来，拾起帽子，出去了。

我把我们掀翻的长凳课桌一一扶正，坐下消消气。歇过劲来，我朝那个圆脑袋小家伙喊——他正抖抖索索站在墙角提着裤子，因为刚才逃命时裤子的扣子全跑丢了：“你对那男孩干了什么？他干吗要打你？”

“我啥也没干，”他答道，一面朝我走来，一面往上提着裤子。

“你叫什么？”

===Original===
“Robert Brown.”

“Where you live?”

“In your village, in that little house near Ou-ni-ja-bi’s.”

“That’s Ne-ma-ha’s house.”

===Chinese===
“罗伯特·布朗（Robert Brown）。”

“你住哪儿？”

“就住你们村，Ou-ni-ja-bi 家旁边那座小屋里。”

“那是 Ne-ma-ha 家的房子。”

===Original===
“Yes, that’s my father.”

And so it was the son of that man for whom I was all bruised up.

Ne-ma-ha was the poorest man in my father’s village, and had no recognition among the prominent men of the tribe, although he had been the priest or hereditary keeper of the sacred tent of war. It was only by the performance of valorous deeds that men won honors in the tribe; but this man had no ambition to win such honors. As a hunter he was also a total failure, consequently his worldly possessions were not such as could give him distinction. Like his brother, who was struck by lightning, he deserted his sacred charge through craven superstitious fear, and, having lost his priestly position, he had become a useless member of the tribe.

“What’s your Omaha name?” I asked, as I pinned his trousers to his suspenders with sharp sticks and nails.

===Chinese===
“对，那是我父亲。”

原来我这一身青一块紫一块，竟是替这么个人家的儿子挨的。

Ne-ma-ha 是我父亲村里最穷的人，在部落头面人物中间毫无地位，尽管他当过战神帐的祭司，也就是那份圣职的世袭守护人。在部落里，人只有靠英勇的作为才能赢得荣誉；可这人无意去挣这样的荣誉。作为猎手他也一无所成，所以他的家私不足以给他挣来体面。像他那个被雷劈过的兄弟一样，他因为怯懦的迷信恐惧丢弃了自己的圣职，丢了祭司的位置之后，他便成了部落里一个没用的人。

“你的奥马哈名字是什么？”我一面用尖木棍和钉子把他的裤子别到背带上，一面问。

===Original===
“They call me Hae-th’naʻ-ta,” he replied, wiping his face with the end of his coat sleeve.

The youngster belonged to the Elk band of the tribe, hence the boy’s name, the English translation of which is, horns forked, meaning the forked-horned elk. How he came by his English name I do not know.

From this time on the lad was always near me, and gradually became my devoted follower. Although at first I did not care for him much, he finally won my friendship by his faithfulness and good nature. He always assisted me as far as his strength would permit in the work assigned me about the school; thus it was that Little Bob, as he was familiarly known, became a satellite to the group to which I belonged, and so safe from the attacks of the other boys.

Brush, Edwin, Warren, Lester, and I were now recognized by all the boys of the school as a gang, and were spoken of as “the Middle Five.” We had fallen into this close companionship without any formal arrangement, and we were regarded as the strongest group between the Big Seven and the other gangs.

===Chinese===
“他们叫我 Hae-th’naʻ-ta，”他答道，用衣袖口擦着脸。

这少年属于部落的麋鹿氏族，他的名字就是打这儿来的；这名字译成英文是“叉角”，意思是长分叉角的麋鹿。他的英文名字是怎么来的，我就不知道了。

打这时起，这孩子总在我身边转，渐渐成了我忠实的跟班。起初我不大看得上他，可他到底凭着忠心和好性子赢得了我的友情。学校分派给我的活计，只要力气够，他总来搭把手；就这样，人见人叫的小鲍勃成了我们这一伙的卫星，别的男孩也就再不敢欺负他。

布拉什、埃德温、沃伦、莱斯特和我，如今被全校男孩公认为一伙，被称为“中间五人”（the Middle Five）。我们并没有经过什么正式的结拜就结成了这份亲密的伙伴关系，大家公认我们是大七人以下、各小帮派之上最强的一伙。

## XII Ponka Boys / 蓬卡男孩

===Original===
“*Woo-hoo! Nooʻ-zhin-ga paʻ-hon ba ma kae donʻ-ba i ga!*”10 exclaimed a new student, ignorant of the rule against speaking Indian.

We scrambled out of bed and rushed to the windows. Sure enough, there was snow on the ground, and the trees that the frost had stripped of their verdant beauty now stood resplendent in a mantle of white.

Summer had gone. The myriads of little creatures that only a short time ago enlivened the hills and valleys had withdrawn into the recesses of the earth, or other places of safety, each according to its own peculiar habit.

Winter had come. And the schoolboy, defying its chilling blasts, dances about in the crisp snow, or on the ice, shouting to his playmates. Delighting in the exercise of every muscle, he races to the hilltop, blows his hot breath on his tingling fingertips, mounts his little sled, then dashes down the hill with merry shouts of laughter, though the snow whirls and flies about his ears and beaming face. Again and again he takes this wild descent until he hears the calling of the school-bell; then, with reluctant feet, he enters the classroom, to study the divisions of the earth either by natural boundaries, or by the artificial ones made by aggressive man, to learn about weights and measures, or to memorize the great events that have changed the conditions of nations and of peoples.

===Chinese===
“Woo-hoo! Nooʻ-zhin-ga paʻ-hon ba ma kae donʻ-ba i ga！” 10 一个新学生喊道，全然不知不许讲印第安语的规矩。

我们手忙脚乱爬下床，冲向窗户。果然，地上落了雪，那些被严霜剥去青翠的树木，如今披着白斗篷，亮得耀眼。

夏天过去了。不久前还叫漫山遍谷生趣盎然的无数小生灵，都按各自的本能退进了大地深处，或别的安稳去处。

冬天来了。学校里的男孩全不怕那砭骨的寒风，在脆生生的雪上、冰上蹦跳着，朝玩伴们呼喊。他浑身筋骨都活动得痛快，一路赛跑到山顶，把滚热的气息呵在刺痛的指尖上，跨上小雪橇，欢笑着冲下山坡，任雪花绕着耳朵和红扑扑的脸乱飞。这样撒野的俯冲他一遍又一遍，直到听见校铃召唤；这才拖着不情愿的脚步走进教室，去学大地的区划——或依天然界线，或依侵略成性的人造界线，去学度量衡，或者去背那些改变了国家和民族命运的大事。

===Original===
Everyone was up and dressed that morning when Graybeard came to the dormitory; and, after repeating our prayer, we hurried down the two flights of stairs, making a noise like thunder. We ran into the yard, where we wrestled for a while, then rubbed our faces and hands with snow.

One of the teachers asked why the boys did so. “All boys do that,” answered Brush. “The old folks tell them to do it, because then their faces and hands won’t get frozen.”

When breakfast was over that morning, and the students had shifted their positions so as to face the centre of the dining-room, and had folded their arms, the superintendent, marking with his forefinger the chapter he had selected to read at the morning worship, looked up and spoke, “We want the boys to learn the use of tools, and to make things for themselves, so we have provided the boards out of which every boy in this school can make a sled for himself. The carpenter will give to any boy who asks, the materials and show him how to use the tools to make his sled. Of course this must be done before the school hour.”

We looked at each other and smiled. The reading of the Scripture and the prayer seemed to us to be unusually long, but at last they came to an end. Then every boy hurried and scurried to the carpenter’s shop. Soon dozens of hammers were going crack, crack, and the saws zip, zip.

===Chinese===
那天早晨灰胡子来宿舍时，人人都已起身穿好；念完祷文，我们轰隆隆冲下两段楼梯。我们跑进场院，扭打了一阵，又拿雪搓脸搓手。

一位教师问男孩们干吗这样。“男孩们都这么干，”布拉什答道。“老人家叫他们这么干，这样脸和手就不会冻坏。”

那天早晨吃过早饭，学生们调换座位、脸朝饭厅中央坐好、抱臂当胸之后，校监用食指点住他选好在晨间礼拜读的那一章，抬起头来开口道：“我们要男孩们学会使用工具，学会自己做东西，所以我们备好了木板，本校每个男孩都能用它给自己做一副雪橇。谁开口要，木工就给材料，还教他怎么用工具做雪橇。当然，这得赶在上课前做。”

我们相视而笑。读经和祷告在我们看来长得反常，可总算到了头。于是每个男孩连滚带爬冲进木工房。转眼间几十把锤子叮叮当当，锯子滋滋作响。

===Original===
“Be careful, boys! Look out for nails, or you will ruin your saws,” said the carpenter, and he smiled good-naturedly as he went on marking the boards for the next applicant.

Suddenly, in the midst of all the din someone exclaimed, “*Hong!*” which is Indian for “Ouch!” and a big boy danced about, shaking his hand violently in the air, then he brought it down and pressed it between his knees, twisting his body into all sorts of shapes, howling the while. The hammering and sawing ceased, and a dozen voices asked, “What’s the matter?” Peter, who was always clumsy in his movements, instead of hitting the nail he was driving, had struck his thumb and smashed it. The traditional “Indian stoicism” was not in him, so he kept up his howling until the carpenter had put on a tobacco poultice and bandaged the injured thumb.

After a lively coasting on our new sleds one afternoon, we were gathered in the schoolroom, and everyone was busy preparing lessons. The arithmetic class was before the blackboard, answering questions put by the teacher.

“Ulysses Grant,” said Graybeard, “suppose the boards, nails, and work upon your sled cost you fifty-five cents, and you sold it to Edwin Stanton for sixty-three cents, what would be your profit?”

===Chinese===
“当心，孩子们！留神钉子，不然锯子就毁了，”木工说着，好脾气地笑笑，接着给下一个来领料的划线。

忽然，在这片嘈杂当中有人喊了一声“Hong！”——印第安话的“哎哟”——一个大男孩蹦着跳起来，把那只手在空中猛甩，然后夹到两膝中间死命挤，身子拧成各种姿势，一路嚎。锤声锯声都停了，十几个声音齐问：“怎么了？”原来是一向手脚笨拙的彼得（Peter），没砸着他正钉的钉子，一锤砸在自己拇指上，砸烂了。传说中的“印第安人的隐忍”他一点没有，嚎个不停，直到木工给他敷上烟草膏、把伤拇指包扎好。

一天下午，我们坐新雪橇痛痛快快滑了一阵之后聚在教室里，人人忙着预备功课。算术班站在黑板前，回答老师的提问。

“尤利西斯·格兰特（Ulysses Grant），”灰胡子说，“假如你的雪橇连木板带钉子带工钱花了你五十五美分，你把它卖给埃德温·斯坦顿卖了六十三美分，你赚多少？”

===Original===
Ulysses moved uneasily, then began counting rapidly with his fingers.

“Stop counting your fingers. Do the sum with your head,” said Graybeard.

Just at this moment something like a shadow appeared at one of the windows, and all faces, except Graybeard’s, turned in that direction. We soon made out that the shadow was the face of an Indian boy with his buffalo robe drawn over his head and spread against the glass to exclude the glare of the sun, so as to give him a better view within the room. His black eyes peered at us, and at every object within sight. The figure withdrew; then we heard a voice speaking in our own language, “Come quick! Come and look at them!”

Soon the windows were darkened by dozens of the queerest-looking heads we had ever seen. Over each face hung two long braids. As the boys pressed their noses against the glass, and wrinkled their brows in trying to see, they made the strangest and most comical of pictures. They pushed and climbed over each other in their eagerness to observe what was going on inside. We could not help laughing at their appearance.

Edwin nudged me and whispered, “They’re Ponka boys; they wear their scalp-locks in front, and they always have two.”

“Don’t they look funny?” shouted a Ponka boy at the middle window. “See, see that one!” and he pointed at Warren; “he looks just like a little owl; his hair stands straight up, and he has such big eyes.”

===Chinese===
尤利西斯局促地动了动，然后飞快地扳起指头数。

“别数指头。用脑子算这道题。”灰胡子说。
