正在这时，一扇窗户上出现了个影子似的东西，除了灰胡子，所有的脸都转了过去。我们很快看清，那影子是个印第安男孩的脸，他把野牛皮袍蒙在头上抵住玻璃，挡开刺眼的阳光，好看清屋里。他的黑眼睛盯着我们，盯着视野里的每一样东西。那张脸退开了；接着我们听见一个声音说我们自己的语言：“快来看！快来看他们！”

很快，几十个我们从未见过的最古怪的脑袋把窗户遮暗了。每张脸旁垂着两条长辫子。男孩们把鼻子挤在玻璃上，皱着眉头使劲往里瞧，构成一幅幅最离奇最滑稽的图画。他们急着想看清里面的动静，互相推搡、叠着往上爬。他们的模样叫我们忍不住直笑。

埃德温捅捅我，悄声说：“他们是蓬卡（Ponka）男孩；他们的头皮辫留在前头，而且总是两条。”

“他们看着多逗啊！”中间窗户那儿一个蓬卡男孩喊。“瞧，瞧那个！”他指着沃伦；“他活像只小猫头鹰；头发直挺挺立着，眼睛又那么大。”

===Original===
Study became impossible, and the class in arithmetic made horrible blunders. Graybeard was disgusted; in vain he rapped the desk with his ruler; and his patience found a limit when Andrew Johnson said that Ulysses’ profit would be eleven cents, if he sold his sled for sixty-three cents. He gave the boy a vigorous shaking. This act of discipline delighted the little savages at the window; they shouted with laughter and the ends of their little braids fluttered with the breath of every peal. They interspersed their merriment with comments on our appearance, our clothing, and the absence of scalp-locks on our heads.

“What are they saying?” asked Graybeard, looking toward the windows.

“They’re calling us names,” answered Warren, who felt sore at being compared to an owl.

Graybeard went to the door; as he opened it, the intruders ran swiftly to the fence, and sat astride of the top board.

===Chinese===
功课没法进行了，算术班错得一塌糊涂。灰胡子烦透了；拿戒尺敲课桌也不管用；等安德鲁·约翰逊（Andrew Johnson）说尤利西斯的雪橇要是卖六十三美分、赚头是十一美分时，他的耐心到了头。他把那男孩狠狠抖了一顿。这番整顿把窗台上的小野蛮人乐坏了；他们放声大笑，两条小辫梢随着每一阵哄笑的呼吸直飘。他们笑着乐着，还不时对我们的长相、衣着和头上没有头皮辫评头论足。

“他们在说什么？”灰胡子望着窗户问。

“他们在骂我们，”沃伦答道，被比作猫头鹰让他很不受用。

灰胡子朝门口走去；他一开门，那些闯来的飞快跑到栅栏边，骑坐在顶栏上。

===Original===
“Get away from here!” said Graybeard, in a loud voice. “Go home!”

“How do do! Goo-by!” shouted back some of the little rascals with boisterous jeers.

“Class in history,” called Graybeard as he closed the door; and a number of us stood in line at the usual place.

“Philip Sheridan, can you tell me something of George Washington?”

All eyes turned toward the youngster who answered to the name of George Washington, and who, neglecting his lessons, was now busy drawing on his slate a caricature of a boy against whom he had a grudge. Hearing his name, and thinking he had been caught in his mischief, he looked up with a startled expression, and rose to make a denial, when Sheridan, fixing his eyes upon him, slowly answered, “He chopped his father’s chokecherry-tree.”

===Chinese===
“走开！”灰胡子大声说。“回家去！”

“你好你好！再见再见！”几个小捣蛋鼓噪着回敬。

“历史课，”灰胡子关上门喊道；我们几个在老地方站成排。

“菲利普·谢里登（Philip Sheridan），你能给我讲讲乔治·华盛顿的事吗？”

所有的眼睛都转向那个应名“乔治·华盛顿”的男孩——他荒废着功课，正埋头在石板上给他记恨的一个男孩画像捉弄。听见自己的名字，他以为恶作剧被抓住了，一脸惊惶地抬起头，站起来正要抵赖，谢里登眼睛盯着他，慢悠悠地答道：“他砍了他爹的野樱树。”

===Original===
The little savages returned to the windows, and began chattering noisily. Suddenly a number of them stood in line, imitating the history class, while one of the big boys took a place before them, mimicking the actions of Graybeard and the tones of his voice, by giving the peculiar rhythm of English to his own Indian words.

“*Ahʻ-bru-zhe-dae!*” he asked; “do you ever wash your face?” And the make-believe class went into fits of laughter.

“Ten sleeps ago,” angrily retorted the boy addressed, “you stole some honey, and the smirches of it are still on *your* face!”

The boys were convulsed at this reply, and so were the boys in the schoolroom; but the mock teacher took a different view of the matter, and sprang at his impudent pupil, boxing his ears, whereat the two fell on each other in a lively tussle. We stretched our necks to see the struggle, and Graybeard also watched the scene.

All at once a Ponka boy shouted, “I’ve found something! Come, come!” and the crowd moved away, leaving the two to finish their wrestling.

===Chinese===
小野蛮人回到窗前，吵吵嚷嚷说开了。忽然，他们中有几个站成一排，学历史班的样子，一个大男孩站到他们前头，模仿灰胡子的动作和腔调，把自己讲的印第安话套上英语特有的节奏。

“Ahʻ-bru-zhe-dae！”他问；“你洗不洗脸？”那装模作样的班级笑得东倒西歪。

“十个睡之前，”被问的男孩怒冲冲回嘴，“你偷了蜂蜜，那脏印子这会儿还在你脸上呢！”

这句回敬让窗外的男孩们笑瘫了，教室里的男孩也笑瘫了；可那位假老师对这事另有看法，扑向他那无礼的学生，左右开弓扇耳光，两人随即扭作一团，打得热闹。我们伸长脖子看这场角力，灰胡子也望着这一幕。

忽然一个蓬卡男孩喊：“我找着东西了！来呀，来呀！”人群呼啦啦走了，撇下那两个继续摔他们的跤。

===Original===
Before long we heard a great clatter in the hallway, and then the Ponka boys were seen marching out of the yard with our sleds. We heard them coasting down the hill, and this made us very restless, so that we could not pay any attention to our lessons. By and by the shouting on the hillside ceased, and Warren leaned over to Brush and whispered, “They’re going off with our sleds!”

Brush raised his right hand; Graybeard saw him, and asked what he wanted.

“Those Indian boys are going away with our sleds, and we want to go after them.”

Permission being given, in a twinkling there were twenty or thirty schoolboys charging up the hill, all mad as hornets. We overtook the Ponkas midway between the school and the village. The little savages turned and came to meet us.

===Chinese===
不久我们听见门厅里一阵乱响，接着就见蓬卡男孩们扛着我们的雪橇列队出了场院。我们听见他们顺着山坡滑下去，急得心痒难熬，功课再也听不进去。渐渐地，山坡上的喊声停了，沃伦俯身对布拉什耳语：“他们要带着咱们的雪橇跑啦！”

布拉什举起右手；灰胡子看见了，问他要什么。

“那些印第安男孩要抢走咱们的雪橇跑了，我们得去追。”

许可一到，一眨眼二三十个学生就嗷嗷叫着往山上冲，个个怒得像马蜂。我们在学校和村子之间追上了蓬卡人。小野蛮人们转过身，迎着我们走来。

===Original===
“What do you want?” said the big boy who had played teacher.

“We want our sleds,” said Brush.

“Come and get them!” was the defiant answer of the Ponka boys.

“That we will do!” answered Brush.

We all moved forward, and then followed a scene hard to describe. A terrific battle took place between us and the robbers; it was hand to hand, and shin to shin, for hands and feet were the only weapons used.

===Chinese===
“你们要干什么？”那个装老师的大男孩说。

“我们要雪橇。”布拉什说。

“来拿啊！”蓬卡男孩们挑衅地答道。

“这就来拿！”布拉什答。

我们全体上前，接下来那场面难以形容。我们和这些抢东西的之间爆发了一场恶战；短兵相接，腿脚相搏，因为拳脚是仅有的兵器。

===Original===
The Ponkas made a determined resistance. I cannot very well relate what happened around me; for I was engaged in a lively bout with an impish-looking little chap for whom I had taken a sudden and unreasonable spite. It was hard to get at him, for he was quick as a wildcat in his movements, and he gave me a number of vicious blows before I could touch him. I noticed that he was more afraid of my brogans than of my fists; taking advantage of this, I pretended to lift my foot for a fierce kick; he hopped backwards, and, in so doing, bent his body toward me. Quick as a flash, I grasped his two braids, pulled his head down, and brought my right knee up against it with tremendous force, and he went sprawling in the snow.

“Frank, Frank, come here, quick!” It was Brush calling. I turned, and there he lay under two of the Ponkas, who were dealing him heavy blows. In a second I had dragged one of them off, and Brush had his footing again. Someone shouted, “They’re running! they’re running!” and the boys we were fighting broke loose. Then all of us schoolboys chased the Ponkas, and drove them into their camp.

We were a bruised lot when we came back to the school; but we had our sleds.

===Chinese===
蓬卡人抵抗得极顽强。我身边的事我说不太清；因为我正跟一个鬼精灵模样的小家伙斗得不可开交，我对他突如其来、毫无来由地起了狠劲。够着他不容易，他动作快得像野猫，我还没沾着他的边，先挨了他好几记狠的。我注意到他更怕我的厚头靴，不及怕我的拳头；我就钻这个空子，装作抬脚要狠命一踹；他向后一蹦，身子却朝我这边一俯。说时迟那时快，我一把攥住他的两条辫子，把他的头拽下来，右膝顶着猛力一撞，他扑通摔进雪里，四脚朝天。

“弗兰克，弗兰克，快过来！”是布拉什在喊。我回头，他正被两个蓬卡人压在底下，挨着沉重的拳头。转瞬我拖开一个，布拉什又站稳了。有人喊：“跑了！他们跑了！”跟我们缠斗的男孩们挣脱跑开。于是我们全校男孩一起追，把他们直赶回营地。

回到学校，我们个个鼻青脸肿；可雪橇都夺回来了。

## Preface / 序言

===Original===
As the object of this book is to reveal the true nature and character of the Indian boy, I have chosen to write the story of my schoolfellows rather than that of my other boy friends who knew only the aboriginal life. I have made this choice not because the influences of the school alter the qualities of the boys, but that they might appear under conditions and in an attire familiar to the reader. The paint, feathers, robes, and other articles that make up the dress of the Indian, are marks of savagery to the European, and he who wears them, however appropriate or significant they might be to himself, finds it difficult to lay claim to a share in common human nature. So while the school uniform did not change those who wore it, in this instance, it may help these little Indians to be judged, as are other boys, by what they say and do.

It is not my purpose to give a continued story with a hero in the following pages, but, in a series of sketches, to present the companions of my own young days to the children of the race that has become possessed of the land of my fathers.

This introduction is a genuine one, for all the boys who appear in these sketches have really lived and played a part in the incidents herein recorded. Each little actor, including the writer, made his entrance upon the stage of life in the “teepee” or in the dome-shaped earth lodge; for, in the years when we boys were born, only the aboriginal dwellings were in use among our people, the Omaha tribe of Indians. Like all the infants for countless generations in the line of our ancestry, we too had to pass through the cradle-board period while our bones “ripened,” as the Indians say, and grew strong enough to bear the weight of our bodies. When at last our mothers gave us liberty to creep and to toddle about, we promptly used that freedom to get into all sorts of mischief as we explored the new and wonderful world in which we found ourselves.

===Chinese===
本书的目的在于揭示印第安男孩真实的性情与品格，因此我选择写的，不是我那些只识得原住民生活的少年朋友的故事，而是我同学们的故事。我作此选择，并非因为学校的影响改变了这些男孩的品质，而是想让他们以读者熟悉的环境和装束出现。油彩、羽毛、皮袍，还有构成印第安人装束的其他物件，在欧洲人眼里都是野蛮的标记；穿戴它们的人，无论这些东西对他自己多么贴切、多么有意义，都难以主张分享一分共同的人性。所以，虽然校服并没有改变穿它的人，但在本书里，它或许能帮助这些小印第安人像别的男孩一样，凭他们的言行受到评判。

我无意在下面这些篇章里讲一个有主人公的连贯故事，而是想借一组素描，把我少年时代的伙伴们，介绍给如今占有了我祖辈土地的那个民族的孩子们。

这篇引子是如假包换的，因为这些素描里出现的男孩们，都确实活过，都在书中记下的事迹里扮演过一角。每个小演员，包括笔者本人，都是在“梯皮”（teepee，尖顶兽皮帐篷）或圆顶土屋这人生舞台上登场的；因为在我们这些男孩出生的年代，我们奥马哈（Omaha）印第安部落中通行的还只是原住的居所。如同我们世系里无数代的婴儿一样，我们也得度过襁褓板上的岁月——趁我们的骨头，照印第安人的说法，还在“长熟”，还没硬到能扛住自己身子的分量。等母亲们终于放我们下地爬、下地蹒跚学步，我们立刻拿这自由去闯种种祸，一路打量我们发现自己身处的这个新奇世界。

===Original===
Among my earliest recollections are the instructions wherein we were taught respect and courtesy toward our elders; to say “thank you” when receiving a gift, or when returning a borrowed article; to use the proper and conventional term of relationship when speaking to another; and never to address anyone by his personal name; we were also forbidden to pass in front of persons sitting in the tent without first asking permission; and we were strictly enjoined never to stare at visitors, particularly at strangers. To us there seemed to be no end to the things we were obliged to do, and to the things we were to refrain from doing.

From the earliest years the Omaha child was trained in the grammatical use of his native tongue. No slip was allowed to pass uncorrected, and as a result there was no child-talk such as obtains among English-speaking children﻿—the only difference between the speech of old and young was in the pronunciation of words which the infant often failed to utter correctly, but this difficulty was soon overcome, and a boy of ten or twelve was apt to speak as good Omaha as a man of mature years.

Like the grown folk, we youngsters were fond of companionship and of talking. In making our gamesticks and in our play, we chattered incessantly of the things that occupied our minds, and we thought it a hardship when we were obliged to speak in low tones while older people were engaged in conversation. When we entered the Mission School, we experienced a greater hardship, for there we encountered a rule that prohibited the use of our own language, which rule was rigidly enforced with a hickory rod, so that the newcomer, however socially inclined, was obliged to go about like a little dummy until he had learned to express himself in English.

===Chinese===
我最早的记忆里，有那些教我们敬老知礼的训导：受了馈赠要说“谢谢”，归还所借的物件也要说；同人说话要用合乎规矩的亲属称谓；绝不可直呼任何人的名字；别人在帐篷里坐着，不先求得允许不得从人家面前走过；还谆谆告诫我们，万万不可盯着来客看，尤其不可盯着生人看。在我们看来，该做的事没个完，不该做的事也没个完。

奥马哈的孩子从牙牙学语起就在母语的规范用法上受训练。任何一个语病都不放过，都得纠正；结果是英语儿童间那种“娃娃腔”我们没有——老少言语之间唯一的差别只在词的发音上，幼儿常念不准，可这难关不久便过，十岁十二岁的男孩说起奥马哈话来，往往不输于壮年男子。

跟大人们一样，我们小孩子也爱做伴、爱说话。做游戏棒也好，玩也好，我们嘴上不停，聊着心里惦记的事；大人谈话时我们只能低声说话，就觉得苦不堪言。进了传教所学校，我们尝到了更大的苦头：那里有条禁令，不许我们说自己民族的话，这禁令靠山核桃木棍严格执行；于是新生不管多么爱交际，也得像个不会说话的小木头人一样走来走去，直到学会用英语表达自己。

===Original===
All the boys in our school were given English names, because their Indian names were difficult for the teachers to pronounce. Besides, the aboriginal names were considered by the missionaries as heathenish, and therefore should be obliterated. No less heathenish in their origin were the English substitutes, but the loss of their original meaning and significance through long usage had rendered them fit to continue as appellations for civilized folk. And so, in the place of Tae-nooʻ-ga-wa-zhe, came Philip Sheridan; in that of Wa-pahʻ-dae, Ulysses S. Grant; that of Kooʻ-we-he-ge-ra, Alexander, and so on. Our sponsors went even further back in history, and thus we had our David and Jonathan, Gideon and Isaac, and, with the flood of these new names, came Noah. It made little difference to us that we had to learn the significance of one more word as applied to ourselves, when the task before us was to make our way through an entire strange language. So we learned to call each other by our English names, and continued to do so even after we left school and had grown to manhood.

The names thus acquired by the boys are used in these sketches in preference to their own, for the reason that Indian words are not only difficult to pronounce, but are apt to sound all alike to one not familiar with the language, and the boys who figure in these pages might lose their identity and fail to stand out clearly in the mind of the reader were he obliged to continually struggle with their Omaha names.

In the talk of the boys I have striven to give a reproduction of the peculiar English spoken by them, which was composite, gathered from the imperfect comprehension of their books, the provincialisms of the teachers, and the slang and bad grammar picked up from uneducated white persons employed at the school or at the Government Agency. Oddities of speech, profanity, localisms, and slang were unknown in the Omaha language, so when such expressions fell upon the ears of these lads they innocently learned and used them without the slightest suspicion that there could be bad as well as good English.

===Chinese===
我们学校的男孩全被起了英文名，因为他们的印第安名字教师们念不上来。再说，传教士们认为那些原住民的名字属于异教，理应抹掉。顶替上去的英文名论出身同样异教十足，只是用得久了，原有的含义和讲究磨掉了，才变得配做文明人的名号。于是，顶了 Tae-nooʻ-ga-wa-zhe 的位置来了菲利普·谢里登；顶了 Wa-pahʻ-dae 的，来了尤利西斯·S·格兰特；顶了 Kooʻ-we-he-ge-ra 的，来了亚历山大，如此等等。我们的教父们还往更古的年代里去翻，于是我们有了大卫和约拿单，基甸和以撒，而随着这一股新名字的洪流，挪亚也来了。摆在我们面前的任务，是闯过一门彻头彻尾的陌生语言；在这种情况下，多学一个用在自己身上的词是什么意思，对我们来说算不了什么。于是我们学会彼此称呼英文名，出了学校、长大成人之后，也一直这样叫下去。

书中素描沿用男孩们这样得来的名字，而不用他们自己的，原因在于：印第安词语不光难念，在不熟悉这门语言的人听来还个个相似；倘若读者得不时同这些男孩的奥马哈名字苦苦纠缠，那么这些篇章里的男孩们就会失去各自的面目，难以在读者心中站立分明。

男孩们的言谈，我力求再现他们说英语的特别腔调；那是一种混合物，采集自他们对书本的一知半解、教师们的乡音土调，还有从学校或政府代理处雇用的未受教育的白人那里学来的俚语和病句。言语的怪癖、亵渎、土话和俚语，奥马哈语里一概没有；所以这些话头一落进少年们的耳朵，他们便天真地学了来、用了去，丝毫不疑心英语里竟还有好赖之分。

===Original===
The misconception of Indian life and character so common among the white people has been largely due to an ignorance of the Indian’s language, of his mode of thought, his beliefs, his ideals, and his native institutions. Every aspect of the Indian and his manner of life has always been strange to the white man, and this strangeness has been magnified by the mists of prejudice and the conflict of interests between the two races. While these in time may disappear, no native American can ever cease to regret that the utterances of his fathers have been constantly belittled when put into English, that their thoughts have frequently been travestied and their native dignity obscured. The average interpreter has generally picked up his knowledge of English in a random fashion, for very few have ever had the advantage of a thorough education, and all have had to deal with the difficulties that attend the translator. The beauty and picturesqueness, and euphonious playfulness, or the gravity of diction which I have heard among my own people, and other tribes as well, are all but impossible to be given literally in English.

The talk of the older people, when they speak in this book, is, as well as I can translate it, that of every day use.

Most of the country now known as the State of Nebraska (the Omaha name of the river Platt, descriptive of its shallowness, width, and low banks) had for many generations been held and claimed by our people as their own, but when they ceded the greater part of this territory to the United States government, they reserved only a certain tract for their own use and home. It is upon the eastern part of this reservation that the scene of these sketches is laid, and at the time when the Omahas were living near the Missouri River in three villages, some four or five miles apart. The one farthest south was known as Tonʻ-won-ga-hae’s village; the people were called “wood eaters” because they cut and sold wood to the settlers who lived near them. The middle one was Ishʻ-ka-da-be’s village, and the people designated as “those who dwell in earth lodges,” they having adhered to the aboriginal form of dwelling when they built their village. The one to the north and nearest the Mission was E-staʻ-ma-za’s village, and the people were known as “the make-believe white men,” because they built their houses after the fashion of the white settlers. Furniture, such as beds, chairs, tables, bureaus, etc., were not used in any of these villages, except in a few instances, while in all of them the Indian costume, language, and social customs remained as yet unmodified.

In those days the Missouri was the only highway of commerce. Toiling slowly against the swift current, laden with supplies for the trading posts and for our Mission, came the puffing little steamboats from the “town of the Red-hair,” as St. Louis was called by the Indians, in memory of the auburn locks of Governor Clark﻿—of Lewis and Clark fame. We children used to watch these noisy boats as they forced their way through the turbid water and made a landing by running the bow into the soft bank.

The white people speak of the country at this period as “a wilderness,” as though it was an empty tract without human interest or history. To us Indians it was as clearly defined then as it is today; we knew the boundaries of tribal lands, those of our friends and those of our foes; we were familiar with every stream, the contour of every hill, and each peculiar feature of the landscape had its tradition. It was our home, the scene of our history, and we loved it as our country.

===Chinese===
白人中间对印第安生活与性格的普遍误解，多半出于对印第安人的语言、思维方式、信仰、理想和本族制度的一无所知。印第安人及其生活方式的方方面面，在白人眼里从来都是陌生的；这份陌生又被偏见的迷雾和两个种族之间的利害冲突放大了。这些或许终有消散的一天；但本族的美国人，永远无法不痛惜这一点：先辈的言语一译成英语就屡遭贬损，他们的思想常被歪曲成笑谈，他们固有的尊严被弄得晦暗不明。寻常的口译员，英文多半是东一榔头西一棒槌学来的，受过完备教育的凤毛麟角，而且人人都得面对译者难逃的种种难关。我在本族人民以及别的部落中听到的那种言辞之美、那种图画般的生动、那种音韵里的谐趣，或者那种措辞的庄重，几乎没法逐字逐句地搬进英语。

书中年长者的谈话，在我力所能及的范围内，都是照他们日常的口吻翻译的。

如今称为内布拉斯加州的大部分地方（“普拉特”河的这个奥马哈名字，描摹的是它水浅、河宽、岸低），许多世代以来一直由我们的人民占有并声称为己有；可当他们把这片疆土的大半割让给合众国政府时，只为自己留出了有限的一块，做日用和家园。这些素描的场景，就安置在这片保留地的东部；其时奥马哈人正临密苏里河居住，分为三个村，彼此相距约四五英里。最南边的叫 Tonʻ-won-ga-hae 村；村里人被唤作“食木者”，因为他们砍柴卖给住在近旁的移民。中间的是 Ishʻ-ka-da-be 村，村里人被指为“住土屋的人”，因为他们建村时仍固守原住民的居式。靠北离传教所最近的是 E-staʻ-ma-za 村，村里人以“假白人”闻名，因为他们照白人移民的式样盖房子。床、椅、桌、柜之类的家具，这些村子里一概不用，例外寥寥无几；而印第安的衣着、语言和社会习俗，在各村都还一仍其旧。

那些年月，密苏里河是唯一的商路。喘着粗气的小轮船从“红发镇”——印第安人对圣路易的称呼，为的是纪念刘易斯与克拉克探险里闻名的克拉克州长那一头红发——逆着急流缓缓而上，满载着商栈和我们传教所的补给。这些闹哄哄的船在浑浊的河水里开路、船头扎进松软的河岸靠岸的情形，我们孩子们常常看个不停。

白人说起这个时期的这片地方，总说那是“一片荒野”，仿佛那是一块没有人类兴味、没有历史的空地。可在我们印第安人看来，它当时就如今日一样界划分明；我们知道部落土地的疆界，知道友邦的、也知道敌邦的；我们熟悉每一条溪流、每一道山冈的轮廓，每一处特别的地貌都有自己的传说。那是我们的家园，是我们历史的现场，我们像爱自己的祖国一样爱它。

## XVI The Break / 诀别

===Original===
“Brush! Brush! Brush!” I ran calling one morning soon after breakfast, down to the barn, to the spring, and back to the house, but I could not find the boy; then I thrust my fingers into my mouth and blew a loud robin call, and the answer came from under a tree up on the hillside. I ran hurriedly to the place; there lay Brush in the shade on the green grass reading.

The occasion of this excited search and call was the announcement by the superintendent that the school would be closed that day, and the children dismissed, so that they might go and see their parents, it being reported by an Indian who had come for his little girl that the people had just returned from the hunt.

“I been everywhere trying to find you,” I said to Brush. “My folks have come home. Put that old book away and come go with me to see them. There isn’t going to be any school today.”

“Frank, it’s right good of you to ask me, but I don’t feel very well; I think I better not go,” he replied, in a tone of disappointment. “All my bones ache, and I don’t know what’s the matter with me; but you go ’long, boy, and have a good time; you can tell me all about your visit when you come back.”

===Chinese===
“布拉什！布拉什！布拉什！”一天早晨，早饭后没多久，我一路喊着找他，跑到马棚，跑到泉水边，又跑回楼里，还是找不见这男孩；我把手指插进嘴里，吹出一声响亮的知更鸟叫，回答从山坡上一棵树底下传来。我急忙跑到那儿；布拉什正躺在树荫的绿草上看书。

我这番急切的寻找呼唤，起因是校监宣布那天停课、放学生们回家看父母——一个来接小女儿的印第安人捎话说，人们刚从捕猎场回来。

“我到处找你，”我对布拉什说。“我家的人回来了。把那本破书收起来，跟我一道去看看他们。今天不上课。”

“弗兰克，难为你来叫我，可我身上不大舒服；我看还是不去的好，”他答道，语气里透着失望。“我浑身骨头都疼，不知道自己是怎么了；你去吧，伙计，好好玩一场；回来把你回家的事讲给我听。”

===Original===
“I’m sorry you can’t go, Brush; but I’ll come back soon and bring you some buffalo meat,” I said, starting to go; “you better think about it again and come.”

“I think I better stay home and be quiet,” he answered, opening his book.

I spent all the forenoon with my parents, and in the afternoon I went in search of some of my village playmates. I found a number of them on the hillside shooting with their bows and arrows. They gave me a noisy welcome in mock English, which made me laugh heartily; then I had to wrestle with one or two of them, and when our peculiar greetings were over, the boys resumed their play, in which they let me join, one of them lending me his bow and arrows.

Our shooting from mark to mark, from one prominent object to another, brought us to a high hill overlooking the ripe fields of corn on the wide bottom below, along the gray Missouri. Here and there among the patches of maize arose little curls of blue smoke, while men and women moved about in their gayly-colored costumes among the broad green leaves of the corn; some, bending under great loads on their backs, were plodding their way laboriously to the fires whence arose the pretty wreaths of smoke.

===Chinese===
“你去不成，我真难过，布拉什；可我很快回来，给你带牛肉，”我说着动身要走；“你再想想，还是来吧。”

“我想我还是待在家里安安静静的好，”他答道，翻开书。

我陪了父母一上午，下午去找村里的一些玩伴。我在山坡上找到好几个，正拿弓箭射着玩。他们用打趣的英语朝我大喊大叫地欢迎，逗得我放声大笑；接着我免不了同一两个摔跤；这一套特别的见面礼行完，男孩们接着玩，也让我入伙，一个还把弓箭借给了我。

我们从靶到靶、从一处显眼的东西射到另一处，一路射到了一座高山上，俯瞰下面谷底沿灰色的密苏里河铺开的熟玉米地。玉米地块之间，这里那里升起一缕缕青烟；男男女女穿着鲜艳的衣裳，在玉米宽大的绿叶间走动；有的背上驮着大捆的东西，弯着腰，一步一挨地朝那升起漂亮烟圈的火堆挪。

===Original===
“They’re making sweet corn,” exclaimed one of the youngsters whose little naked brown back glinted against the afternoon sun, and he pointed to the workers in the field.

As we stood watching the busy, picturesque scene below us, one little fellow held his bow close to his ear and began strumming on the string, then all the rest played on their bows in the same manner, until one of them suddenly broke into a victory song, in which the others joined.

At the close of the song they gave me a graphic description of the attack on the camp when it was pitched on the Republican river. Although the enemy was repulsed, and the hunting ground secured to our people, the battle cost many lives, several of the enemy’s warriors were left on the field, and the Omahas lost some of their bravest men.

While yet the boys were telling of the thrilling incidents of the battle, we arose with a sudden impulse and rushed down the hill with loud war-cries, as though attacking the foe, the tall grass snapping against our moccasined feet as we sped along. We were rapidly approaching a house which stood alone, when one of the older boys who was running ahead suddenly stopped and raised his hand as though to command silence. Immediately our shouts ceased, and, seeing the serious look on the lad’s face, “What is it?” we asked in frightened tones as we gathered about him.

Without a word he pointed to a woman who was cutting the tall sunflower stalks that had almost hidden her little dwelling with their golden blossoms. Her long black hair flowed over her shoulders unbraided, a sign of mourning. Now and again she would pause in her work to look up at the humble home and utter sighs and sobs that told a tale of sorrow. Mingled with these outpourings of grief came often the words, “My husband! my little child!” with terms of endearment and tenderness for which I can find no equivalent in English. On a blanket spread over the ground near by sat a tot of a child babbling to itself and making the beheaded sunflowers kiss each other, innocently oblivious of its mother’s grief. It was a sad homecoming for the woman; the spirit of her husband had fled to the dark clouds of the west to join the host of warriors who had died on the field of battle, and his bones lay bleaching in the sands of a far-off country.

===Chinese===
“他们在做甜玉米，”一个光着棕色小背的少年喊道，脊背在午后的太阳下闪闪发亮，他指着地里干活的人。

我们正站在那儿看脚下这幅忙碌的画景，一个小家伙把弓竖到耳边，拨起弦来；接着别的孩子也都照样拨起弓弦，直到其中一个忽然唱起一支凯歌，其余的跟着唱了起来。

唱罢，他们给我绘声绘色地讲起大营扎在共和河（Republican river）上时遭袭的那一仗。敌人虽被打退，猎场也保给了我们的人，这一仗却赔上了许多性命：敌人的几名武士倒在了战场上，奥马哈人也折了几员最勇的战士。

男孩们还在讲着战役里惊心动魄的情节，我们忽然一齐心血来潮，呐喊着战呼冲下山去，仿佛进攻敌人，鹿皮鞋踏过之处，高草噼啪直响。我们飞快逼近一座孤零零的房子，跑在前头的一个大男孩猛地站住，举起手来示意肃静。喊声立刻停了；见那少年脸色郑重，我们围拢上去，惶惶地问：“怎么了？”

他一言不发，指了指一个女人。她正在砍高高的向日葵秆，那一片金灿灿的花盘几乎把她的小屋整个遮住了。长长的黑发散披在肩上，不编辫子——那是居丧的标记。她干一会儿活，便停下抬头望望那寒酸的家，发出声声叹息抽泣，道出满腹悲苦。哀哀的倾诉里，时时夹着这样的话语：“我的丈夫！我的小孩子！”还夹着一些爱怜温存的称呼，我在英语里找不出对等的词。近旁摊在地上的毯子上坐着一个奶娃娃，咿咿呀呀自言自语，让砍了头的向日葵互相亲嘴，浑然不知母亲的悲伤。对这女人来说，这是一场伤心的还家：丈夫的魂灵已经飞往西天的黑云，汇入战死疆场的武士群中，他的尸骨正在遥远国度的沙地里晒得发白。

===Original===
“It is Gre-don-ste-win weeping for her husband who was killed in the battle last summer,” whispered the big boy; “let us go away quietly.”

When we had withdrawn to a distance where we were sure our noise would not disturb the mourner, one of the boys called out, “Let’s play *Oo-haeʻba-shon-shon*!”13 Years after I learned that this game was played by the children of the white people, and that they called it, “Follow my leader.”

We graded ourselves according to size, the biggest boy at the head as leader. Each one took hold of the belt of the boy in front of him, and then we started off at a rapid jog-trot, keeping time to this little song which we sang at the top of our voices.

Children’s Song

“Follow My Leader.”

Whatever the leader did, all were bound to do likewise. If he touched a post, we touched it too; if he kicked the side of a tent, all of us kicked it; so on we went, winding around the dwellings, in and out of vacant lodges, through mud puddles and queer, almost inaccessible places, and even entering the village, where we made the place ring with our song.

===Chinese===
“是 Gre-don-ste-win 在哭她丈夫，去年夏天那一仗里死的，”大男孩悄声说；“咱们悄悄走吧。”

我们退到一定的距离，确信吵闹不会再搅扰那位哀哭的人了，一个男孩喊道：“玩 Oo-haeʻba-shon-shon 13 吧！”多年以后我才知道，白人的孩子也玩这游戏，他们管它叫“跟着领队走”。

我们按个头大小排好，最大的男孩领头做队长。每人抓住前面男孩的腰带，然后就小跑着出发，一面用最大的嗓门齐声唱着这支小曲，合着节拍。

儿童歌曲

《跟着领队走》

队长做什么，众人就得跟着做什么。他碰一根柱子，我们也碰；他踢一下帐篷边，我们个个踢；我们就这样绕着一座座住屋蜿蜒前行，钻进钻出空着的屋子，蹚过泥塘，翻过奇奇怪怪几乎无处下脚的地方，甚至一路进了村，把歌声唱得满村回响。

===Original===
At last, tired out, the boys broke line and scattered to their homes. It was then that I suddenly realized the lateness of the hour, and remembered my promise to Brush. I ran to the house, took a hurried leave of my parents, picked up the package of buffalo meat my mother had prepared for my schoolmate, and fairly flew over the hill between the village and the Mission.

As I came running down the hill to the school I saw Lester, Warren, and Edwin sitting in a row on the fence.

“Hello!” I shouted, “what you sitting on that fence for, like a lot of little crows?”

No answer came, nor did the boys move. I began to wonder if they were displeased with me, although I could not think of anything I had done to give them offence. As I drew near, I noticed that the expression on their faces indicated alarm rather than displeasure, and, becoming anxious in my turn, I hurriedly asked, “What’s the matter; what’s happened; where’s Brush?”

The boys looked at one another, then at me; finally Lester replied, almost with a sob, “Brush is awful sick; he’s been raising blood; they sent for the Doctor.”

===Chinese===
最后，男孩们跑乏了，散了队，各自回家。就在那时我才猛然觉察时辰已晚，想起对布拉什的许诺。我跑回屋里，匆匆辞别父母，拎上母亲给同学备好的牛肉包，翻过村子和传教所之间的山冈，简直像飞一样。

我朝学校跑下山坡时，看见莱斯特、沃伦和埃德温在栅栏上一溜坐着。

“喂！”我喊道，“你们坐在栅栏上干什么，活像一排小乌鸦？”

没人答话，男孩们也不动。我开始疑心他们生了我的气，虽然想不起自己干过什么得罪他们的事。走近了，我看出他们脸上的神情是惊慌，不是不满；我自己也慌了，急忙问：“出什么事了？怎么了？布拉什呢？”

男孩们面面相觑，又看看我；末了莱斯特几乎是哽咽着答道：“布拉什病得厉害；他咳血了；他们去请大夫了。”

===Original===
“Where is he? I must go see him,” I said, springing over the fence, and starting toward the house.

“He’s in that little room next the girls’ playroom; but they won’t let anybody see him,” said Warren.

I went to the room in which Brush lay, and knocked very gently on the door. There was a rustling movement inside, then the door slowly opened and one of the lady teachers stood before me.

“What is it, Frank?” she asked in a low tone.

I tried to look over her shoulder to see the bed, but she was too tall. “I want to see Brush; can’t I see him? They say he is sick. I want to see him a moment,” I pleaded. “I’m just come back from the village, and brought some buffalo meat I promised him.”

===Chinese===
“他在哪儿？我得去看看他，”我说着翻过栅栏，朝楼里奔去。

“他住在女孩游戏室旁边那间小屋里；可谁也不让见。”沃伦说。

我走到布拉什躺着的房间门口，极轻地敲了敲门。里面一阵窸窣，门缓缓开了，一位女教师站在我面前。

“什么事，弗兰克？”她低声问。

我努力想从她肩上望见那张床，可她太高了。“我想看看布拉什；不能让我看看吗？他们说他病了。我就看一眼，”我恳求道。“我刚从村里回来，给他带了答应过的牛肉。”

===Original===
“No, Frank, you cannot see him,” was the reply. “He is very sick. The superintendent is with him trying to relieve his suffering. Run away now,” said the lady, stroking my bare head with her small hand. “Don’t make any noise, and tell the rest of the boys to be very quiet.”

I went away reproaching myself for not coming back from the village soon, as I told Brush I would. When I rejoined the boys, they looked anxiously into my face, and Edwin asked, “Did you see him?”

“No, they would not let me.” After a pause, I asked, “When did he get sick; who was with him?”

“It was under the walnut-tree,” said Lester; “he was reading to us about Joseph, out of his little black Bible he always carries. He began to cough hard and choke; he dropped the book all covered with blood, and took hold of my brother’s arm. I ran to tell the superintendent. Just as they carried Brush into the house, Edwin came back and we told him about it.”

In the evening, after the small boys had gone to bed, the doctor came, a tall gray-haired man. At the gate he was met by the superintendent, and the two walked slowly up the steps, talking earnestly. We four had been watching for the doctor on the porch; as he came along we caught now and then a word, but we did not understand its meaning. We judged by the shaking of the doctor’s head that he thought Brush’s case was serious.

===Chinese===
“不行，弗兰克，你不能见他，”她答道。“他病得很重。校监正陪着他，想法子减轻他的痛苦。快走吧，”女士说着，用她小小的手抚了抚我的光头。“别弄出声响，也告诉其他男孩们要非常安静。”

我走开了，一路责怪自己没有照对布拉什说过的那样早点从村里回来。我回到男孩们那里，他们焦急地打量我的脸，埃德温问：“你见着他了吗？”

“没有，他们不让我见。”顿了顿，我问：“他什么时候病的？当时谁跟他在一起？”

“就在核桃树底下，”莱斯特说；“他正给我们念约瑟的故事，用他总带在身边的那本小黑圣经。他突然咳嗽、气噎；书掉在地上，溅满了血，他一把抓住我哥哥的胳膊。我跑去告诉校监。他们把布拉什抬进屋的时候，埃德温刚回来，我们把这事告诉了他。”

晚上，小男孩们睡下之后，大夫来了，是个高个子灰白头发的人。校监在大门口迎住他，两人一面恳谈一面慢慢走上台阶。我们四个一直在廊子上盼着大夫；他走过时，我们断断续续听见几个词，可不懂什么意思。从大夫摇头的样子判断，我们认为布拉什的病不轻。

===Original===
Days passed; the doctor came and went; yet Brush’s door was closed to us, nor had we any hopeful news of him. We missed him sadly; we missed his whittling, his harmless scolding; and our play was only halfhearted.

Indians who came to the school on business missed his ready offer of help. There was no one to take his place; no one who could interpret for them as well as he. Each one, as he went away, left a word of cheer for the lad, with expressions of hope for his recovery.

As school was dismissed one afternoon, the teacher gave special injunctions to the scholars not to make any noise as they passed out, or while moving about the house, so as not to disturb the sick boy. We four strolled toward the spring. Frost had come, and the leaves were beginning to turn red and yellow. Wild geese flew noisily overhead, fleeing from the coming winter to sunnier climes. While we were counting, as we often did, the gray birds, floating through the air like a great V, Warren suddenly exclaimed, “Say, boys, plums!”

We looked at him inquiringly. “Let’s go get plums for Brush!” he continued excitedly. Then we remembered that we had preempted a small grove of choice plum bushes at the head of the ravine, as against all the boys of the school, and acquired a right in it which even the Big Seven respected.

Edwin ran to the kitchen and borrowed from one of the cooks a small tin pail. We hurried to our orchard, where we saw no signs of trespass; the bushes were laden with beautiful ripe fruit. We filled the little pail with the choicest, then each one picked for himself. It was nearly suppertime when we appeared at Brush’s door. The three boys looked at me; so I tapped very gently, and the teacher who was nursing the sick boy opened the door.

===Chinese===
一天天过去；大夫来了又走；布拉什的门始终对我们关着，也没有关于他的好消息。我们想他想得慌；想他削木头的声响，想他那不伤人的数落；玩也玩得无精打采。

来学校办事的印第安人，也都惦记不上他那一声热心的“我来帮忙”了。没人顶得上他的缺；没人能像他那样给他们传话。每个人临走，都给这少年留下一句话打气，盼他早日康复。

一天下午放学时，教师特意叮嘱学生们，出门时、在楼里走动时都不要出声，免得打扰那病中的男孩。我们四个信步走向泉水。霜降了，树叶开始转红转黄。野雁吵吵嚷嚷从头顶飞过，逃离将至的冬天，去晒更暖的太阳。我们正像往常那样数着那些灰鸟——它们像一个大大的 V 字浮在空中——沃伦忽然喊道：“哎，伙计们，李子！”

我们询问地望着他。“咱们给布拉什摘李子去！”他兴奋地接着说。这下我们想起，早在沟谷顶头那一小片上好的李树丛，我们就对全校男孩宣示了主权，在那片树上挣下了一份连大七人都敬重的权利。

埃德温跑到厨房，向一个厨娘借了只小锡桶。我们赶到自己的果园，不见有人来犯的痕迹；树丛上坠满了熟得好看的李子。我们拣最好的装满小桶，然后每人又给自己摘了一份。等我们出现在布拉什门口，天都快开晚饭了。三个男孩看着我；于是我把门极轻地叩了叩，看护病孩的教师开了门。

===Original===
“We’ve brought some plums for Brush,” I said, offering the tin pail.

“That’s very nice,” said the lady, softly; “I will give them to him.” She was about to close the door, when I whispered, “Can we take just a little look at him?”

“Yes,” she answered, throwing the door open.

We four leaned forward and looked in. A smile lit up Brush’s face as he saw us. “How are you now?” I asked, in a loud whisper.

“I’m all right,” he whispered back, although his hollow eyes and cheeks told a tale that stole away all our hopes. We withdrew, and the door was slowly closed.

===Chinese===
“我们给布拉什带了些李子，”我说着递上锡桶。

“太好了，”女士轻声说；“我拿给他。”她正要关门，我小声说：“能让我们看他一小眼吗？”

“能，”她答着，把门敞开。

我们四个探身往里看。布拉什看见我们，脸上亮起一个微笑。“你现在怎么样？”我大声低语问。

“我挺好，”他小声答，可他深陷的眼窝和两颊讲述的故事，把我们的希望偷了个精光。我们退出来，门缓缓关上了。

===Original===
Next morning as I was coming down from the dormitory I paused at Brush’s door to listen. I heard footsteps moving about softly, then the door opened and one of the big girls came out with a white pitcher in her hand. I started to go on downstairs, when she called to me in a whisper, “Frank, go down to the spring and get some fresh water for Brush, will you, that’s a good boy?”

I took the pitcher and went quietly downstairs. As soon as I was outside the yard, I ran as hard as I could to the spring, glad at the prospect of a chance to see my friend again. Warren and Lester met me as I was coming up the hill.

“Did you see him?” one of them asked.

“No, but I’m going to,” I answered.

“Ask him if we can do anything for him?” said Lester.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨我从宿舍下来，在布拉什门口停下听动静。我听见轻轻走动的脚步，随后门开了，一个大姑娘手里拿着白水壶走出来。我正要接着下楼，她低声唤住我：“弗兰克，去泉边给布拉什打点新鲜水来，好孩子，好不好？”

我接过水壶，悄悄下了楼。一出院门，我就撒腿朝泉水飞奔，巴不得又有机会见我的朋友一面。我上山时，沃伦和莱斯特迎面碰上我。

“你见着他了吗？”其中一个问。

“没有，可我就要去见。”我答。

“问问他我们能不能为他做点什么？”莱斯特说。

===Original===
Just as I reached the head of the stairs the same big girl appeared. I handed her the pitcher; she took it and was about to enter the room, when I caught her arm. “Just let me take a look at Brush, will you?” I whispered.

“No, Frank, I can’t. Superintendent says to let nobody in.”

I heard a cough, then a feeble voice say pleadingly, “Maria, let him in, just a minute!”

The girl looked cautiously around, then said to me, “Come, but don’t let anybody see you. Don’t stay long, be quick!”

I stepped in, and a thin hand was stretched out to receive me. “I can’t talk much, I’m so weak,” said Brush. Overcome with emotion, I could not speak but stood holding his hot hand. The girl at the door moved uneasily.

===Chinese===
我刚到楼梯口，还是那个大姑娘出现了。我把水壶递给她；她接过正要进屋，我一把拉住她的胳膊。“就让我看布拉什一眼，行不行？”我悄声说。

“不行，弗兰克，不行。校监说了，谁也不让进。”

我听见一声咳嗽，随即一个微弱的声音恳求道：“玛丽亚（Maria），让他进来，就一分钟！”

姑娘小心地四下看看，然后对我说：“来吧，可别叫人瞧见。别待久，快点！”

我走进去，一只瘦削的手伸出来迎我。“我说不了多少话，太虚弱了。”布拉什说。我激动得说不出话，只是攥着他滚烫的手站着。门口的姑娘不安地挪动着。

===Original===
“Tell the boys I’m all right,” said Brush. “They mustn’t worry. Come nearer.” I bent over him and he whispered, “Tonight, when everybody is asleep, come down and see me. I want to talk to you when I’m alone.”

As night came on we four sat under the walnut-tree watching Brush’s window. A candle was lit, then the curtain was drawn. Below in the dining-room, the large girls moved quietly to and fro, busy with their evening work. When this was finished, they gathered at the door, and softly sang that beautiful hymn, “Nearer my God to Thee.” We joined in the chorus, the wind waiting the words to the broad skies. The singing came to a close; the dining-room lights were put out, and we were called to bed.

As we knelt by the side of our beds to repeat the Lord’s prayer, I could not keep back the tears that came, thinking of the emaciated little form that I was to see once more that night.

One by one the boys fell asleep, and I alone, among the forty or fifty in that big room, remained awake. The clock down in Graybeard’s room struck eleven; the only sounds that came to my ears were those of the heavy breathing of the boys, the soughing of the wind through the trees, the rushing of the waters in the river, and now and then the calls of the wild geese, migrating in the night.

The clock struck the hour of twelve; I sat up listening. There was a stir and the sound of a voice that startled me. It was only Warren moving and talking in his sleep. I went stealthily to the head of the stairs, then listened again. I could only hear the throbbing of my heart, and the rasping pulsations in my ears. After a pause which seemed interminable, I put one foot down the first step, the board sprang under my weight, and creaked. Again I paused to listen; there was no stir, and I went on. Every little sound in the stillness of the night seemed exaggerated, and I was often startled, but I went on and reached the door of Brush’s room. I scratched the panel three times. There was a movement within, and a slight cough. Slowly I turned the knob and opened the door. I entered, closed the door, but left it unlatched.

===Chinese===
“告诉伙伴们我挺好，”布拉什说。“叫他们别担心。再过来点。”我俯下身，他悄声说：“今晚上，等大家都睡了，下来见我。我要趁一个人的时候跟你说话。”

夜幕降临，我们四个坐在核桃树下，望着布拉什的窗。一支蜡烛点亮了，随后窗帘拉上。下面饭厅里，大姑娘们安静地来来去去，忙着晚间的活计。忙完了，她们聚到门口，轻声唱起那首好听的圣歌《与主亲近》。我们跟着唱副歌，风把歌声送往辽阔的夜空。唱罢，饭厅的灯熄了，有人喊我们睡觉。

我们跪在床边念主祷文时，想到今夜还要再见一面的那具枯瘦的小身形，眼泪怎么也忍不住。

男孩们一个个睡着了，那间大屋里的四五十个人里，只剩我一个醒着。楼下灰胡子房间里的钟敲了十一点；我耳朵里只有男孩们沉重的呼吸、风穿过树木的呜咽、河里的流水声，还有间或掠过的夜里南迁的野雁的鸣叫。

钟敲十二点；我坐起来听。一阵响动、一个声音吓了我一跳——原来是沃伦在翻身说梦话。我蹑手蹑脚挪到楼梯口，又听。只听见自己的心跳和耳中轰鸣的脉搏。挨过仿佛没有尽头的停顿，我把一只脚踩上第一级楼梯，木板在我身下弹起来，咯吱一响。我又停下听；没有动静，我接着走。夜深人静，一点点声响都像被放大了，我不时受惊，还是一路走到了布拉什的门口。我在门板上轻轻挠了三下。里面一阵响动，一声轻咳。我慢慢转动门钮，开了门。我进去，把门掩上，没有上闩。

===Original===
A candle stood burning in the midst of a number of bottles on a little table near the head of the bed. I knelt by the bedside, and Brush put his arm around my neck. We were silent for a while, finally he whispered in the Omaha tongue:

“I’m glad you came; I’ve been wanting to talk to you. They tell me I am better; but I know I am dying.”

Oppressed with ominous dread, I cried out, interrupting him, “Don’t say that! Oh, don’t say that!”

But he went on, “You mustn’t be troubled; I’m all right; I’m not afraid; I know God will take care of me. I have wanted to stay with you boys, but I can’t. You’ve all been good to me. My strength is going, I must hurry﻿—tell the boys I want them to learn; I know you will, but the other boys don’t care. I want them to learn, and to think. You’ll tell them, won’t you?”

He slipped his hand under the pillow, brought out his broken-bladed jackknife, and put it in my hand, then said, “I wish I had something to give to each one of the boys before I go. I have nothing in the world but this knife. I love all of you; but you understand me, so I give it to you. That’s all. Let me rest a little, then you must go.”

===Chinese===
床头小桌上，一堆瓶子中间立着一支燃烧的蜡烛。我跪在床边，布拉什伸胳膊搂住我的脖子。我们沉默了一会儿，末了他用奥马哈语悄声说：

“你来了我真高兴；我一直想跟你说话。他们说我好些了；可我知道我要死了。”

不祥的恐惧压得我受不了，我喊出声来，打断他：“别这么说！哦，别这么说！”

可他接着说：“你别难过；我没事；我不怕；我知道上帝会看顾我。我本想跟你们兄弟们待在一起，可待不成了。你们个个都对我好。我的力气正在走，我得抓紧——告诉伙伴们，我要他们用心学；我知道你会的，可别的男孩不上心。我要他们学，要他们想。你会告诉他们的，对不对？”

他把手滑到枕头底下，摸出他那把折了刃的小刀，放到我手里，然后说：“真想在走之前给每个伙伴留点东西。这世上我除了这把刀什么也没有。我爱你们每一个；可你懂我，所以我把它给你。就这些。让我歇一会儿，然后你就得走了。”

===Original===
After a moment’s stillness the door opened very gently, and the floor near it creaked as though there were footsteps. A breath of wind came and moved the flickering flame of the candle round and round. The boy stared fixedly through the vacant doorway. There was something strange and unnatural in his look as, with one arm still around me, he stretched the other toward the door, and, in a loud whisper, said, “My grandfather! He calls me. I’m coming, I’m coming!”

There was a sound as of a movement around the room; Brush’s eyes followed it until they again rested upon the open door, which swung to with a soft click; then he closed his eyes.

I crept closer to the sick boy; I was quivering with fear. Brush opened his eyes again, he had felt me trembling. “Are you cold?” he asked.

Just then I heard footsteps in the girls’ playroom; this time they were real; Brush heard them too.

“Superintendent,” he said with an effort.

---

When I crept into my bed the clock below struck one. For a long while I lay awake. I could hear noises downstairs, Graybeard’s door open and close, and the door of Brush’s room. I heard a window raised, then everything became still.

---

We did not know how fondly we were attached to Brush, how truly he had been our leader, until we four, left alone, lingered around his grave in the shadowy darkness of night, each one reluctant to leave.

The Mission bell rang for evening service, and with slow steps we moved toward the school﻿—no longer “The Middle Five.”

===Chinese===
静了片刻，门被极轻地推开，门边的地板咯吱作响，仿佛有脚步。一阵风吹来，把摇曳的烛焰吹得直转。那男孩直勾勾望着空空的门口。他的神情有些古怪、不像人间，一只胳膊还搂着我，另一只朝门口伸去，大声低语道：“我爷爷！他在叫我。我来了，我来了！”

屋里响起一阵像是走动的声音；布拉什的眼睛追着它，直到重新落回那扇敞开的门上——门轻轻一响，自动合上了；随后他闭上了眼睛。

我朝病中的男孩凑得更近；我吓得直哆嗦。布拉什又睁开眼，他觉出我在发抖。“你冷吗？”他问。

就在这时，我听见女孩游戏室里响起脚步声；这一回是真的；布拉什也听见了。

“校监。”他费力地说。

---

我爬回床上时，楼下的钟敲了一点。好一阵我醒着。我听得见楼下的动静，灰胡子的门开了又关，还有布拉什房间的门。我听见一扇窗子推起，随后一切归于寂静。

---

直到我们四个孤零零地留在他坟旁、在昏黑的夜色里流连不去、谁也舍不得先走的时候，我们才知道自己把布拉什依恋得多么深，才知道他真是我们的领队。

传教所的晚礼拜钟响了，我们拖着步子朝学校走去——再不是“中间五人”了。

## I The Mission / 传教所

===Original===
Leaning against the wall of a large stone building, with moccasined feet dangling from a high wooden bench on the front porch, sat a little boy crying. His buckskin suit, prettily fringed and embroidered with porcupine quills of the brightest colors, indicated the care bestowed upon him by fond parents. Boys and girls were at play around the house, making the place ring with their merry laughter as they chased each other among the trees, but the little boy sat all alone, sobbing as though his heart would break. A big boy came and sat by his side, put an arm around him, and in a kindly tone said, in Indian:

“What are you crying for? Don’t cry﻿—I’ll play with you and be your friend. I won’t let the boys hurt you.”

“I want my mother! I want to go home!” was all the homesick little chap could say, crying harder than ever.

“You will see your mother soon, we can go home every bathing-day.1 It is only three days to wait, so don’t cry. I have to go away, but I will be back soon. Play with this dog until I come,”﻿—putting into the hands of the little boy a wooden dog.

===Chinese===
一座大石头房的前廊里，高高的木长凳上坐着一个小男孩，穿鹿皮鞋的脚悬空晃荡，正倚着墙哭。他那身鹿皮衣裳缀着漂亮的流苏，绣着色彩最鲜亮的豪猪鬃，可见疼爱他的父母在他身上花了多少心思。男孩女孩们绕着屋子玩耍，在树木间互相追逐，笑声把满院都震响了；可小男孩独自坐着，抽抽搭搭，哭得心都要碎了。一个大男孩走过来坐在他旁边，伸出胳膊搂住他，用印第安语和气地说：

“你哭什么呀？别哭——我跟你玩，做你的朋友。我不会让男孩们欺负你。”

“我要妈妈！我要回家！”这想家的小家伙只会说这一句，哭得比先前更凶。

“你很快就能见着你妈妈，每个沐浴日 1 都能回家。只要等三天，别哭了。我得走了，可很快就回来。我回来之前，跟这条狗玩吧，”——说着把一只木头狗放进小男孩手里。

===Original===
A bell rang, and from every direction came boys and girls, crowding and pushing one another as they entered two of the large doors of the building. The big boy came running, and grasping the little one by the hand, fairly dragged him along, saying: “Come, quick! We are going to eat.”

They entered a large room filled with people. Parallel to the walls stood tables of great length, at one of which the two boys took seats. After considerable hard breathing and shuffling by the children, they suddenly became very still, everyone bowed his head, then a man with gray hair and whiskers, who sat at the end of one of the tables, spoke in a low tone. He finished speaking, then followed a deafening clatter of a hundred tin plates and cups. Young women carrying great pans of steaming food moved rapidly from table to table. One of these girls came to the two boys, and put into the plate of the younger a potato. “Give him two, he’s hungry,” whispered the big boy to the girl.

Everything was strange to the little newcomer and he kept looking all around. The lamps that were fastened to the walls and posts, the large clock that stood ticking gloomily on a shelf, and the cupboard with its tin door perforated in a queer design were objects upon which his eyes rested with wonder.

The supper over, the boys and girls who sat on the inner side of the tables turned to face the centre of the room, and folded their arms. Then they all sang. When this was done, they dropped on their knees and the gray-haired man began to talk again. The little boy watched him for a while, then laid his head on the hard bench﻿—the tones of the old man grew fainter and fainter until the boy lost all consciousness of them. Suddenly there burst upon him a noise like thunder. He arose to his feet with a start, and, bewildered, he looked around. Everything seemed to be in a whirl. He took fright, ran to the door that first caught his sight, and went with a thud down to a landing, but did not lose his balance; he took another step, then fell headlong into a dreadful dark place. He screamed at the top of his voice, frightened almost into a fit. A woman picked him up and carried him in her arms up a flight of stairs, speaking to him in a language that he could not understand.

===Chinese===
铃响了，男孩女孩们从四面八方涌来，推推搡搡挤进大楼的两扇大门。大男孩跑回来，一把攥住小的那只手，简直是拖着他走，嘴里说：“来，快点！我们要吃饭了。”

他们进了一间坐满了人的大屋子。贴着墙摆着几张长桌，两人在其中一张坐下。孩子们呼哧呼哧喘着、挪了好一阵，忽然齐刷刷静下来，人人低下头，随后一个头发胡子花白、坐在一张桌子尽头的男人低声说起话来。他说完了，紧跟着便是一百只锡盘锡杯震耳欲聋的脆响。年轻姑娘们端着一大盘一大盘冒热气的吃食，在桌间飞快穿梭。一个姑娘来到两个男孩跟前，往小的盘里放了只土豆。“给他两个，他饿，”大男孩悄声对姑娘说。

这新来的小家伙看什么都新鲜，眼睛四处打转。钉在墙上和柱子上的灯、搁板上一座阴沉沉滴答作响的大钟、那扇锡门板上打着古怪花纹镂孔的碗柜，都叫他看得出了神。

晚饭吃完，坐在桌子里侧的男孩女孩们转身面向屋子中央，抱起胳膊。然后大家唱歌。唱完，他们跪下去，灰白头发的男人又开始讲话。小男孩望了他一会儿，便把头搁在硬邦邦的长凳上——老人的声音越来越远、越来越远，男孩全然听不见了。忽然一声炸雷般的巨响劈到他头上。他惊得一骨碌站起来，晕头转向地四下张望。样样东西都在打转。他吓坏了，朝最先撞进眼里的一扇门跑去，咚的一声跳落到一个平台上，居然没有摔倒；再迈一步，便一头栽进一个可怕的黑窟窿里。他放开嗓子尖叫，几乎吓抽了过去。一个女人把他抱起来，揽在怀里上了一截楼梯，一路说着他听不懂的话。

===Original===
This was my first experience at the boarding school established by the Presbyterian Board of Foreign Missions for the instruction of the children of the Omaha tribe of Indians.

---

The Mission school, the founding of which had marked an epoch in the tribe, was located among the wooded bluffs of the Missouri on the eastern part of the reservation. The principal building was of stone, plain and substantial, and plastered inside and out. It was three stories high and had an attic. This attic was perhaps the most interesting part of the structure, for we boys were quite sure it was tenanted by ghosts, and that the devil, who figured considerably in the instruction given us, had full sway in this apartment.

There was a large square hole close to the head of the stairs that led up to the attic. This hole had the greatest terror for us; there was a constant whistling within it, and out of it came sounds like distressing moans and sighs. I remember once, when Graybeard had sent me up to the attic for something, that I never hurried so on any other errand as I did on that one. I found the article he desired, put it under my arm, and cautiously approached the head of the stair, keeping an eye on the dark hole, then suddenly I made a dash past it, and with amazing rapidity thundered downstairs. “Lad, you will break your neck!” exclaimed Graybeard. I told him I liked to run downstairs!

===Chinese===
这是我在那所寄宿学校的第一番经历——学校是长老会海外传道理事会（Presbyterian Board of Foreign Missions）为教育奥马哈印第安部落的孩子而设的。

---

传教所学校的创立在部落里划出了一个时代；它坐落在保留地东部密苏里河畔林木葱茏的断崖间。主楼是石头的，朴素结实，里外都抹了灰。高三层，带一个阁楼。这阁楼也许全楼数它最有意思，因为我们男孩们认定里头住着鬼，而那个在我们所受的教导里频频出场的魔鬼，在这套房间里是说一不二的。

通阁楼的楼梯口旁边有一个方方的大窟窿。这窟窿让我们怕到了极点；里头总有哨音，还传出像愁苦的呻吟和叹息的声音。记得有一回灰胡子打发我上阁楼取东西，我从来没有在哪趟差事上跑得像那一趟那么急。我找到他要的东西，往胳膊底下一夹，一面盯住那黑窟窿，一面小心挪到楼梯口，然后猛一下冲过去，以惊人的速度雷鸣般奔下楼。“小子，你想摔断脖子啊！”灰胡子喊道。我告诉他我爱跑着下楼！

===Original===
Under the attic was the boys’ dormitory. The beds were placed close together, and some were wide enough for three boys. The room was large, and in the middle of it stood a post. I have reason to remember this, for one night I got up in my sleep and ran with all my might against this post, making such a noise as to awaken Graybeard and the superintendent, who came up in great haste with candles in their hands. I was laid up for days after this exploit, but I never ran in my sleep again.

Beneath our dormitory were the parlor and the bedroom of Graybeard, our teacher and disciplinarian. This name was not inherited by him, nor was it one of his own choosing; the boys gave it to him because his beard was iron-gray, and the Indians adopted it from the boys. In his room at night he might have heard strange noises from the cherubs in the dormitory above, in fact he came up there quite often, rod in hand, as a reminder that such sounds made sleep impossible.

Under Graybeard’s rooms was the schoolroom where we struggled with arithmetic, geography, history, and A.B.C., up to the Fifth Reader. This room corresponded in size to our dormitory, but it had no middle post.

The dining-hall, where on my arrival I had taken fright and stampeded head foremost into the cellar, was in the middle of the first story. It was very large and held, beside the three long tables, a big stove in the middle between two large posts. I remember these posts very well, I kept close company with one of them, on my return from a runaway expedition; and it was on this occasion that I had my first love adventure﻿—but I must not anticipate.

===Chinese===
阁楼底下是男生宿舍。床一张挨一张摆着，有几张宽得够睡三个男孩。屋子很大，正中立着一根柱子。这柱子我有理由记得清清楚楚：一天夜里我梦中起身，撒腿朝它全力撞去，闹出的动静把灰胡子和校监都惊醒了，两人手里举着蜡烛匆匆赶上楼来。这场壮举之后我躺了好几天，可从此再没有梦游跑过。

宿舍再往下是客厅和灰胡子——我们的教师兼训导——的卧室。这名字不是他承袭来的，也不是他自己挑的；是男孩们给他起的，因为他的胡子是铁灰色，印第安人又从男孩们嘴里把它接了过去。夜里他在自己屋里，兴许能听见头顶宿舍里小天使们古怪的动静；事实上他也常上去，手里拎着戒尺，提醒大家：这种声响叫人没法睡觉。

灰胡子的房间底下是教室，我们在那儿同算术、地理、历史和字母表搏斗，一直搏斗到《第五读本》。这屋子跟宿舍一般大，只是中间没有柱子。

饭厅在二楼正中——就是我初来那天受了惊、一头冲进地窖的那间。屋子很大，除了三张长桌，中间两根大柱子之间还立着一只大火炉。这几根柱子我记得真真切切：我从逃学远征回来之后，同一根柱子亲近了好一阵；也正是那一回，我有了头一场恋爱奇遇——可我不能提前说破。

===Original===
The rooms on the two stories above the dining-hall were occupied, one as playroom for the girls, the others by the various employees.

On the same floor with the schoolroom and the dining-hall, at the north end of the building, was the chapel. Here we sat in rows on Sunday mornings, afternoons, and evenings, and on Thursday evenings, ranged on long, high, wooden benches without backs, our feet scarcely touching the floor, and listened (sometimes) to sermons which were remarkable for their length and sleep-enticing effects. I had many delightful dreams in this chapel, about Samson and his jawbone war club, the fight between David and Goliath, and of the adventures of Joseph the dreamer﻿—stories that were the delight of my boyhood. Brush, one of my dearest friends at the school, knowing my weakness, secured a seat back of mine on purpose to support me when I was in a slumberous mood. I shall never forget his goodness; he now sleeps in the cemetery just above the Mission.

The two large rooms over the chapel were occupied by our superintendent and minister. Above his apartments was the girls’ dormitory, while over all stretched the haunted, ghostly attic.

There were other buildings grouped around: to the back stood the storehouse and the smokehouse; out of the latter came our delicious hams and our sermons, for a part of this building was used as the minister’s study. Then there was the great barn where we boys played hide-and-seek in the haymow; the corncrib with its yellow wealth showing between the boards; and the dusty wheat-bins with padlocked doors. Below on the bottom were the Government saw and grist mills, where we often went to see the grinding of the Indians’ grain and the large trees sawed into lumber for Agency use or for the Indians’ houses. The carpenter and blacksmith shops were also down there, and a long wooden house for the occupancy of the Government employees. All of these buildings stood for the fulfilment of the solemn promises made by the “Great Father” at Washington to his “Red Children,” and as a part of the price paid for thousands and thousands of acres of fine land.

===Chinese===
饭厅上面两层的房间，一间做了女孩们的游戏室，其余由各色雇工住着。

同教室和饭厅一层、在楼北头的是礼拜堂。星期天早、午、晚和星期四晚上，我们在这儿一排排坐在长长的、高高的、没有靠背的木长凳上，脚够不着地，听（有时候听）那些以冗长和催眠著称的布道。我在这礼拜堂里做过许多美梦，梦见参孙和他那驴腮骨战斧，梦见大卫和歌利亚之战，梦见做梦想家约瑟的奇遇——这些都是我少年时代的心爱故事。布拉什——我在学校最亲爱的朋友之一——知道我这毛病，特意弄了个坐我身后的位子，好在我犯困的时候撑我一把。他的好意我永远忘不了；如今他长眠在传教所上方的墓园里。

礼拜堂上面的两大间住着我们的校监兼牧师。他的房间再往上，是女生宿舍；而压在这一切之上的，是那闹鬼的、阴森森的阁楼。

四周还散布着别的房子：屋后立着库房和熏房；我们的喷香火腿和我们的布道词都出自后者，因为那房子的一部分用作牧师的书房。还有大马棚，我们男孩常在草料棚里捉迷藏；玉米仓，金黄的收成从板缝里露出来；落满灰的小麦柜，门上挂着挂锁。下面谷底是政府的锯木厂和磨坊，我们常去看印第安人的谷子磨面，看大树锯成木板，供代理处使用或给印第安人盖房。木工房和铁匠铺也在下面，还有一座雇给政府雇员们住的长长的木屋。这一切建筑，都代表着华盛顿的“大父亲”对他“红孩子”们许下的庄严诺言的兑现，也是成千上万英亩良田所付代价的一部分。

===Original===
Although there were high hills just back of the school, from which one could get excellent views of the surrounding country, we boys preferred to go up into the belfry on the top of the main building for our observations. We did not go often; two difficulties were in the way: the securing of permission from the superintendent, as but few boys could be trusted up there; and we must go through the haunted attic to get to the belfry. No boy during my school days ever went up there alone.

My friend Brush, being quite a favorite with the superintendent, often had permission to go, and took me with him. When we were once in the belfry, we felt safe from the annoyances of the devil and the other horrible things in the attic. The superintendent, without the asking, let Brush have a big spyglass, which the other boys were not permitted to use, and with it we could see far beyond the river and the valley that stretched in the distance to the opposite bluffs, that were always nearly hidden in a bluish haze. Bringing the glass to a closer range, we could see below, on our side of the river, the rich fields of the Mission and of the Indians; and we used to watch the Indians and the hired men of the school at work there. Sometimes we caught sight of a steamboat far down the river coming up, trailing a long line of smoke; then, with great excitement, we would run down and tell the boys, and all of us would hasten to the highest point near the school and watch the “mystic boat” as it slowly made its way along the winding stream.

To the south of the Mission, overlooking the Missouri and a small lake, stood the highest bill for miles around. This was known by the Indians as “the hill on which Umʻ-pa-ton-ga2 was buried.” He was one of the greatest chiefs of the Omahas.

---

===Chinese===
学校背后虽有几座高山，登上去能把四野尽收眼底，我们男孩却更爱上主楼顶上的钟楼去眺望。我们并不常去；有两重难处：一要获校监准许，因为信得过的男孩没几个；二是上钟楼必得穿过那闹鬼的阁楼。我上学那些年，没有哪个男孩敢独自上去。

我的朋友布拉什很得校监欢心，常获准上去，便带着我一道。一旦进了钟楼，我们就觉得不怕阁楼里魔鬼和那些可怕东西的搅扰了。校监不用开口讨，就让布拉什用那副大望远镜——别的男孩一概不许碰——透过它，我们能望过河，望过远处一直铺到对岸断崖的河谷，那断崖常年几乎藏在一层蓝霭里。把镜子调近了看，能看见我们这边河岸下传教所和印第安人的沃田；我们常看印第安人和学校雇工在地里干活。有时我们望见河的下游远远开上来一艘轮船，拖着长长一线烟；于是我们激动地跑下去报信，大家一起赶到学校近旁的最高点，看那条“神秘的船”沿着蜿蜒的河道慢慢行来。

传教所以南，俯临密苏里河和一个小湖，立着方圆数英里最高的山。印第安人管它叫“安葬 Umʻ-pa-ton-ga2 的山”。他是奥马哈人最伟大的酋长之一。

---

===Original===
Before schools of any kind were known among the Omahas, Indian parents warned their boys and girls against a free association with the children of persons who did not bear a good character. “Who was that you were playing with?” a father or mother would ask. “Nobody knows the child’s family﻿—beware of him, do not go with him, he will throw upon you the habit of lying or stealing. Go with children whose parents are respected by the people.” Such advice would be given by the reputable men and women of the tribe to their children as to choosing their playfellows.

At the school we were all thrown together and left to form our own associates. The sons of chiefs and of prominent men went with the sons of the common people, regardless of social standing and character. The only distinction made was against cowardice; the boy who could not fight found it difficult to maintain the respect of his mates, and to get a place among the different gangs or groups of associates the boys had established among themselves. I learned this from my friend Brush, to whom I complained one day of being abused by the boys when he was not near. “You must look out for yourself now,” he said. “If the boys know you won’t fight, they will tease you all the time. You must fight.”

So the next boy who rudely shoved me aside and knocked my hat off received a painful surprise, for my right fist came so hard against his cheekbone that he stood for a moment as though stunned. Then he moved, and I moved, and the boys standing near could hardly tell which was which until we separated, pretty well bruised. After that the boys were careful not to knock my hat off my head; if they did, they took pains to let me know that it was not intentional.

===Chinese===
在奥马哈人还不知学校为何物的年代，印第安父母就告诫自家儿女，不要同名声不好的人家的孩子随便来往。“方才跟你玩的是谁？”做父亲或母亲的会问。“没人知道那孩子的家底——防着他点，别跟他走，他会把撒谎偷东西的习气带给你。要跟父母受人敬重的孩子来往。”部落里正经人家的父母择取儿女玩伴时，给的就是这样的教导。

在学校里，我们全被扔在一处，各人自去找各人的伴。酋长和头面人物的儿子同平民百姓的儿子混在一起，不讲门第，不讲人品。唯一讲究的是对怯懦的歧视：不会打架的男孩很难保住伙伴们的敬重，也难挤进男孩们自己结成的各帮各派。这是我从朋友布拉什那儿学来的；有一天我向他抱怨，说我不在他跟前时净挨男孩们欺负。“如今你得自己当心了，”他说。“男孩们要是知道你不肯打，就老是作践你。你得打。”

于是下一个粗鲁地把我搡到一边、打掉我帽子的男孩，就吃了场痛的意外：我的右拳结结实实撞上他的颧骨，他愣在那儿像被打懵了。随后他动，我也动，站近旁的男孩们几乎分不清哪个是哪个，直到我们分开，两人都鼻青脸肿。打那以后，男孩们小心着不再打掉我的帽子；万一打了，也赶紧表白不是故意的。

===Original===
I told Brush about this set-to, and he approved of it. “That’s right,” he said; “fight any of them, even if you know that you’re going to get licked; then they won’t tease you.”

My father was the principal chief of the tribe and leader of the village of the “Make-believe white-men;” he had plenty of horses, the standard of Indian wealth, yet that did not entitle me to a place in any of the different gangs in the school; I had to show that I was not afraid to stand up and fight. Even good-natured Brush had to bristle up at times and engage in a lively tussle, else there would have been no peace for him. Now I was wanted by the smaller gangs and invited by them to their places of sport; but Brush held on to me and kept me out.

Among the boys there was the gang of the “Big Seven” which Brush had been trying to enter; but, for some reason which I did not then understand, they would not admit him. He did not care to go into any of the gangs of smaller boys, of which there were quite a number. I thought the “Big Seven” did not want him, because he was too small; but later I found out there was another reason for it.

As time passed, I learned more and more of the peculiar ways of the boys at the school, of the teachers, and of my books. It was not long before I felt quite at home and independent; but Brush and I were still without a gang.

===Chinese===
我把这场架讲给布拉什听，他很赞成。“这就对了，”他说；“跟他们谁都可以打，哪怕明知道要挨揍；这样他们就不作践你了。”

我父亲是部落的大酋长、“假白人”村的头人；他马匹成群——那是印第安人的财富标准——可这并不能让我在学校任何一帮一派里占个位子；我得亮出我不怕站起来打。连好脾气的布拉什有时也不得不竖起毛干上一场热闹的扭打，不然就休想安生。这下那些小帮派都想拉我，请我去他们的游乐场；可布拉什攥着我不放，把我挡在外面。

男孩们中间有个“大七人”帮，布拉什一直想进去；可不知为什么——那原因我当时不明白——他们不肯收他。比他小的男孩们的帮派倒有不少，他一个也不屑于进。我原以为“大七人”不要他，是因为他个头太小；后来才知道另有缘故。

日子一天天过去，我对学校男孩们、教师们和书本的种种门道越学越精。没多久我就觉得如鱼得水、无拘无束；可布拉什和我仍旧没有帮派。

## XIII The Secret of the Big Seven / 大七人的秘密

===Original===
The small boys had been marched to bed at eight o’clock. We, the Middle Five, who, for the first time, were permitted to stay up until ten﻿—a privilege hitherto enjoyed only by the Big Seven﻿—sat around the fire listening to Indian tales told by Edwin in his animated way. There was no light in the room save that which came through the open door of the stove, in front of which the storyteller had taken his place. The flickering fire cast a ruddy light upon the fine features of the boy, and the shadows on the wall danced to the caprice of the restless flames. We laughed heartily at the mishaps of Ish-teʻ-ne-ke, a comical character that figures in the folktales of the Omahas, as they were vividly portrayed in language and gesture.

Outside the wind was moaning and sighing through the trees around the house, at times rattling the windows vigorously, as though threatening to rush in upon us; and from the neighborhood of the graveyard came the mournful sounds of the hooting owl.

In the back part of the schoolroom, where it was dark, sat the Big Seven, carrying on an earnest conversation in low tones, as though to exclude us from their confidence.

The leader of this gang was a youth of peculiar appearance and manner. He was tall and muscular, with prominent nose and cheekbones. Although he took an active part in the amusements and sports of the school, often inaugurating them himself, we never knew him to change the expression of his face, either in pain, anger, or mirth. We five often had talks about the peculiarities of this singular youth. Brush said that “Aleck” (the boys addressed him by this name, for he was called after the Macedonian conqueror) was turned outside in, that all his laugh, anger, and sorrow were inside and couldn’t be seen. Edwin declared that the boy had ceased laughing since the killing of his father by the Sioux, and that he was reserving his laugh for the time when he should take revenge.

===Chinese===
小些的男孩八点就被押去睡觉了。我们中间五人，头一回获准熬到十点——这特权先前只有大七人享有——围着炉火坐着，听埃德温用他活灵活现的方式讲印第安故事。屋里没有灯，光亮只有炉门里透出来的那一点，讲故事的人就坐在炉门口。跳动的火光给这男孩清秀的脸庞涂上一层红，墙上的影子随着不安分的火焰起舞。我们被伊什特尼卡（Ish-teʻ-ne-ke）的倒霉事逗得放声大笑——那是奥马哈民间故事里的滑稽角色，让他连说带比划地演活了。

屋外，风绕着房子四周的树木呜咽叹息，时时狠狠摇响窗子，像要闯进来吓唬我们；墓园那边，传来猫头鹰哀哀的啼声。

教室后部黑着的地方坐着大七人，压着嗓门热烈地商量着什么，像是有意把我们排除在他们的信任之外。

这帮人的头领是个相貌举止都格外特别的青年。他高壮结实，鼻梁颧骨凸出。学校的游乐和运动他样样参加，还常常是发起人，可无论疼痛、发怒还是高兴，我们谁也没见他变过脸色。我们五个常议论这个古怪青年的特异之处。布拉什说，“亚历克”（Aleck）（男孩们这么称呼他，因为他是照那位马其顿征服者起的名）是里外翻了个个儿，他的笑、他的怒、他的愁都翻在里面，外头看不见。埃德温则宣称，自从他父亲被苏族（Sioux）人杀死，这男孩就不再笑了，他把笑声攒着，留到报仇的那一天。

===Original===
The mysterious consultation in the back part of the schoolroom came to an end, and one by one the Big Seven approached the stove and mingled with us. Aleck, who was the last, did not sit down in the space left for him, but drew up a desk and perched on one end of it, resting his feet on the bench where he should have sat. He leaned over, supporting his body with his elbow on his knee, and shaded his eyes with his hand. We could feel that for some purpose he was looking into the faces of the Middle Five.

As the Seven took their places among us, Edwin brought his story to a close, and we fell into silence. After a few moments Aleck cleared his throat, and, without change of attitude, said in the Omaha language, fearlessly breaking one of the rules of the school:

“Boys, to you of the Five I speak. There is not a gang in the school that has not its secrets. You of the Five have yours, no doubt; we of the Seven, who now sit with you, have ours. We respect yours, and we have every confidence that you respect ours. Ordinarily we do not interfere with each other’s affairs; but now that you have the same privilege that we have had, and we are thrown together, we of the Seven think that your gang should unite with ours in a secret that up to this time has been ours alone, and share in its pleasures. Are you willing to join in it?”

“Yes,” answered Brush, knowing as the rest of us did, what this secret was; “we are willing.”

===Chinese===
教室后头那场神秘的商议结束了，大七人一个接一个凑到炉边，同我们混坐在一起。亚历克走在最后，没有坐进给他留出的空位，而是拖过一张课桌，蹲踞在桌头一端，脚踩在他本该坐的凳子上。他俯下身，肘拄膝盖撑住身子，用手搭在眼上遮光。我们感觉得到，他是带着什么目的，在端详中间五人的一张张脸。

七人在我们中间落座后，埃德温把故事收了尾，我们静了下来。过了一会儿，亚历克清了清嗓子，姿势不变，用奥马哈语开了口，全然不怕破坏校规：

“伙伴们，我对你们五人说话。学校里的帮派没有一家没有自己的秘密。你们五人有你们的，想必没错；我们七人——如今同你们坐在一起的——有我们的。我们尊重你们的，也完全信得过你们尊重我们的。平日里我们互不干涉彼此的事；可如今你们既然享有了我们一直享有的特权，我们又坐在了一处，我们七人认为，你们这一伙应当同一个至今只属于我们的秘密联合起来，分享它的乐趣。你们愿意入伙吗？”

“愿意，”布拉什答道，我们其余人跟他一样，早知道这秘密是什么；“我们愿意。”

===Original===
“You of the Seven, are you satisfied with the answer?”

There was silence. “Then,” continued the leader of the Seven, “I must have the answer of each one of the Five.”

Brush again signified his assent, and the rest of us followed. Having arrived at a mutual understanding which awakened in each one a fraternal feeling, there ensued among all the boys a lively chattering. When the fervor of the friendly demonstration abated, Aleck, in his deep voice, said, “*Waʻ-tha-dae shu-geʻha!*”11

Immediately there was silence, and each one held his breath expectantly, for we recognized the ritual words of “the Leader” in the game, “Obeying the Command,” words which had been sacred to generations of boys who had preceded us.

===Chinese===
“七人的伙伴们，这个回答你们满意吗？”

一片沉默。“那么，”七人的头领接着说，“我必须听到五人每个人的回答。”

布拉什再次表示同意，其余人跟着一一应允。达成了这份在每个人心里唤起兄弟之情的默契之后，男孩们之间顿时热闹地聊开了。等这阵友爱的热乎劲平息下去，亚历克用他低沉的嗓音说了声：“Waʻ-tha-dae shu-geʻha！” 11

顿时鸦雀无声，个个屏息期待，因为我们听出了“遵奉号令”（Obeying the Command）这游戏里“领队”的套语——这些字眼，在我们之前世世代代的男孩听来都是神圣的。

===Original===
“Those are the very words,” whispered Edwin to me; “now listen, hear where the Command will come from, and where it will go.”

“The Word of Command approaches,” continued Aleck, with unmoved face; “from the head of the Ne-shu-de12 it comes, wrapped in a black cloud, the mantle of thunder, like the mighty whirlwind it comes; the great trees of the pine-clad mountains bend to its fury; its voice echoes through the valleys, and the animals, big and little, tremble with fear. On it comes, sweeping over the wide plains; the angry lightnings dart from the cloud; it approaches the village of the Ponkas, at the mouth of the Niobrara, passes it and continues its course down the Ne-shuʻ-de; now it has come to the pictured rocks; it reaches the bluffs of the Cut-lake; but on it comes, swifter and swifter it comes; it is now at the old Omaha village, at the graves of the little ones; it comes﻿—it is here!”

There was a pause, and we all waited in suspense. Just then the wind rattled the windows and the owl up in the graveyard hooted.

“George!” called the leader, in a solemn tone.

===Chinese===
“正是那几个字，”埃德温悄悄对我说；“现在听着，听号令从哪里来，往哪里去。”

“号令来了，”亚历克面不改色地接着说；“它从 Ne-shu-de 12 的源头而来，裹在一朵乌云里，披着雷霆的斗篷，像一股狂烈的旋风而来；披松的青山上，大树在它的暴怒下弯腰；它的声音在山谷间回荡，大大小小的走兽吓得发抖。它一路扑来，扫过辽阔的平原；愤怒的闪电从云中乱窜；它逼近蓬卡人的村子，在尼奥布拉拉河口，一掠而过，继续顺着 Ne-shuʻ-de 而下；如今它到了图岩；它抵达断湖的断崖；可它还在来，越来越快地来；它此刻到了老奥马哈村，到了孩子们的坟地；它来了——它到了！”

停了片刻，我们都悬着心等。恰在这时，风摇响了窗子，墓园里的猫头鹰叫了。

“乔治！”头领庄严地点名。

===Original===
“Present!” promptly responded George in English, as though answering Graybeard’s roll-call. A ripple of suppressed laughter spread among the boys. Aleck, I doubt not, was giggling inside.

“Edwin!” continued the leader, in the same tone.

“Ah-ho!” said Edwin, giving the response and imitating the voice of a grown-up and serious warrior.

“The Word of Command is before you two,” continued Aleck, “the Leader;” “and it is, that soon after Graybeard has gone to bed you are to go to the village and enter the house of Haeʻ-sha-ra-gae, where you will see a woman making pemmican. You will say to her, ‘Woman! we are the commanded and the bearers of the word of Command. Of you we demand a bag of pemmican. Give willingly, and you shall go beyond the four hills of life without stumbling; there shall be no weariness in the pathway of life to hinder your feet, and your grandchildren shall be many and their succession endless!’ Fail not in your mission. Your way out of the house shall be through one of the windows in our dormitory, and by a rope.”

===Chinese===
“到！”乔治用英语应声答道，就像应灰胡子的点名。男孩们中间漾起一阵强忍的笑。亚历克，我毫不怀疑，正在肚子里乐呢。

“埃德温！”头领用同样的调子接着点。

“啊嗬！”埃德温应道，学着大人才有的、郑重其事的武士腔。

“号令降临你们二人面前，”亚历克接着说，“内容是：灰胡子睡下之后不久，你们要进村，走进 Haeʻsha-ra-gae 的家，会看见一个女人在做干肉饼。你们对她说：‘女人！我们是奉命者，是号令的传递者。我们向你讨一袋干肉饼。甘愿地给吧，你便可以走过人生的四座山冈而不绊跌；人生之路再没有困顿拦你的脚，你的孙辈将多不胜数、代代无穷！’切勿误了使命。你们出屋的路，要走我们宿舍的一扇窗，用一根绳。”

===Original===
“It is bedtime, boys, come right up,” called Graybeard, from the head of the stairs. “See that the large doors are bolted.”

When we were in bed, Graybeard went softly downstairs, and we heard him open his door, close it, then lock it. Some of the youngsters were still awake, and, when they heard the closing of Graybeard’s door, began to talk. It seemed as though they would never stop and go to sleep, so that we could carry out the Word of Command. After a while Aleck thought of a plan, and started a game often played by small boys at night; he said, loud enough for the little boys to hear, “*Thaʻ-ka!*” Brush and the rest of us repeated the word, one after the other, and each of the wakeful little fellows, according to the rules of the game, was obliged in his turn to utter the word, and then there was silence, for no one can speak after he has said the word. Soon heavy breathing among the little ones gave sign that they had entered the land of dreams.

It was near the middle of the night when one by one the members of the Big Seven and the Middle Five noiselessly arose. George tiptoed to a corner and brought out a large coil of rope. We went with it to the window directly over that of Graybeard’s bedroom. I do not know why we selected that window, the only dangerous one in the dormitory, but there seemed to be a fatality about it. Very softly the window was raised; George slipped the noose at the end of the rope around his body, then climbed through the window. Slowly we let him down the three stories to the ground. Then we hauled the rope up again, and let Edwin down in the same manner. We closed the window, leaving space enough for the rope, which remained dangling.

On entering the village, the two boys were met by a pack of noisy curs that snapped and snarled at their heels. As the dogs became bolder in their attacks, the lads struck right and left with the heavy sticks they carried; one dog limped away yelping, and another lay thumping his tail on the ground, stunned.

===Chinese===
“睡觉时间到了，孩子们，马上上来，”灰胡子在楼梯口喊。“记得把大门闩上。”

我们上了床，灰胡子轻步下楼，我们听见他开门、关门、上锁。有几个小家伙还没睡着，听见灰胡子关门，就说起话来。他们好像永远不肯停下睡去，好让我们去执行号令。过了一会儿，亚历克想出个主意，起了个小男孩夜里常玩的游戏；他提高了足以让小男孩们听清的嗓音，说：“Thaʻ-ka！”布拉什和我们其余人一个接一个跟着念这个词，每个醒着的小家伙按游戏规矩轮到自己也必须念；随后便静了，因为念过这个词的人谁也不能再说话。很快，小家伙们沉重的呼吸表明他们进了梦乡。

将近半夜，大七人和中间五人的成员一个个悄无声息地起了床。乔治踮脚到墙角，抱出一大盘绳子。我们拎着它走到正对灰胡子卧室窗户的那扇窗前。我不知道我们为什么偏挑那扇——全宿舍唯一危险的一扇——可它好像就是拴着我们的命。窗子被轻轻推起；乔治把绳头的套索套上身子，翻出窗去。我们缓缓把他放下三层，放到地面。然后把绳收上来，照样把埃德温放下去。我们掩上窗，留出过绳的缝，绳就那么悬着。

一进村，两个男孩就被一群乱叫的野狗迎住，狗朝着他们的脚跟又扑又咬。狗胆子越咬越大，两个少年抡起随身的大棍左右开打；一条狗瘸着腿嗥叫着跑了，另一条躺在地上直拍尾巴，被打懵了。

===Original===
The door of the house designated by the leader of the Big Seven squeaked loudly on its rusty hinges as the boys swung it open without the ceremony of knocking. A woman at work in one corner of the room looked up at them, smiled good-naturedly, and said in a sympathetic tone:

“Such a dark night as this! On what errand do they come, and little White-chests, too?”

Four men were sitting on the floor around a flickering candle playing a game; they too looked up at the sound of the door.

“Oho!” said the man of the house, who was one of the players, “for a long time you have not entered my dwelling on a visit; I fear you will make it rain! Walk around the stove and break the charm.”

“Don’t mind him,” said the woman, kindly; “tell me what you want. Won’t you sit down?”

===Chinese===
大七人头领指明的那户人家的门，在锈蚀的合页上被两个男孩推得吱呀大叫——他们没有敲门的客套。屋角一个正做活的女人抬头望他们，和气地笑笑，语气怜爱地问：

“这么黑的天！他们是干什么来的，还是两个小白胸人？”

四个男人围着一支摇曳的蜡烛坐在地上赌牌；听见门响，他们也抬起头来。

“哦嗬！”男主人——牌局中人之一——说，“好久没有来我屋里做客了；只怕你们要给我带来雨！绕着炉子走一圈，破了这道邪。”

“别理他，”女人和气地说；“告诉我你们要什么。坐呀？”

===Original===
The two boys stood hesitating, then George began in a sepulchral voice, “Woman, we are the commanded, the bearers of the Word of Command. We come to demand of you a bag of pemmican. Give plenty﻿—”

“Willingly,” corrected Edwin, in a whisper.

“Willingly, and you shall go beyond the four hills of life without﻿—without﻿—”

“Stumbling,” prompted Edwin.

“Stumbling; there shall be no weariness in the pathway of life﻿—” and so on to the end of the ritual.

===Chinese===
两个男孩迟疑地站着，随后乔治用一种从坟里出来的嗓音开了腔：“女人，我们是奉命者，是号令的传递者。我们来向你讨一袋干肉饼。多多地给——”

“甘愿地，”埃德温小声纠正。

“甘愿地给，你便可以走过人生的四座山冈而不——不——”

“绊跌，”埃德温提词。

“绊跌；人生之路再没有困顿——”如此这般，一直念到底。

===Original===
The woman clapped her hands, and shouted with laughter, as she exclaimed, “If your cloud and lightning and thunder do all you say they will do, they have more power than I supposed they had! Sit down and wait a while, and I will have some pemmican ready for you.”

“Did those old White-chests teach you all that?” asked the husband. “If they did, they have been stealing the rituals of some of our priests, and﻿—”

“Oh, let them alone!” said the wife; “they came to see me.”

“They came in without knocking on the door; that’s bad luck!” the husband continued in his banter; “before entering a house they should knock, as the White-chests do.”

“Be careful, and don’t spill it!” said the wife, as she handed a bag to George, who thanked her.

“There they come!” said one of the Seven in a loud whisper, as he felt a tug on the rope that was tied to his arm.

===Chinese===
女人拍着手放声大笑，喊道：“要是你们的云啊、闪电啊、雷啊真像你们说的那样灵，那它们可比我想的有法力多了！坐下等一会儿，我给你们备干肉饼去。”

“这一套都是那些老白胸人教你们的？”丈夫问。“要是他们教的，那他们就是偷了咱们哪个祭司的仪轨，而且——”

“哦，随他们去吧！”妻子说；“他们是来看我的。”

“他们进门不敲门；那可不吉利！”丈夫接着打趣；“进屋之前该先敲门，就像白胸人那样。”

“拿好，别撒了！”妻子一面把一袋干肉饼递给乔治，一面说；乔治谢了她。

“他们来了！”七人中的一个感到拴在自己胳膊上的绳被拽了一下，大声低语道。

===Original===
We hastened out of bed, being careful not to make any noise. George and Edwin sent the bag of pemmican first, then they were each pulled up and safely landed.

We had built a fire in a vacant room adjoining our dormitory; into this warm room we repaired with our bag, and sat in a circle on the floor, Indian fashion. On a little table stood the one candle allowed us, shedding a feeble light. Two of the boys had stolen down to the dining-room for plates. Alexander, before whom the bag was placed, divided the pemmican equally, while we listened to George and Edwin’s account of their adventure. The plates were passed around; I put out my hand to help myself from my plate, when a member of the Big Seven stopped me. “Wait,” said he; “there is something more to be done.”

Aleck looked up; we all became silent; then he took a tiny bit of the pemmican, and held it toward the sky for a moment as a thank offering to Wakonda, then placed it with great solemnity on the floor in the centre of the circle. This done, we fell to eating, telling stories as we feasted, and had one of the most enjoyable nights of our lives.

From time to time through the winter we had these nocturnal banquets, taking turns in going to the village for our supplies; but misfortune overtook us before the season was fairly over.

One dark night we had our meeting as usual, and the Word of Command came to Lester and to Joel of the Big Seven. When the small boys had gone to sleep, we brought out our rope and let Joel down through the window. Then we put the noose around Lester and proceeded to lower him.

===Chinese===
我们赶紧下床，小心不出一点声响。乔治和埃德温先把干肉饼袋送上来，然后两人被一一拉上，稳稳落进屋来。

我们早就在宿舍隔壁一间空屋里生好了火；我们抱着袋子进了这间暖屋，照印第安人的样子席地围坐成一圈。小桌上立着获准点的那一支蜡烛，洒下微弱的光。两个男孩溜下楼去饭厅偷了盘子。袋子摆在亚历山大面前，他把干肉饼均分成份，我们一面听乔治和埃德温讲他们的历险。盘子传了一圈；我伸手要去拿自己那份，一个大七人拦住了我。“等等，”他说；“还有一道没行完。”

亚历克抬起眼；我们都静下来；他拈起一小撮干肉饼，朝天举了片刻，作为献给瓦孔达（Wakonda）的谢礼，然后极庄重地把它放在圈子中央的地板上。礼成，我们便大嚼起来，一面吃一面讲故事，度过了平生最痛快的一夜。

整个冬天，我们隔三差五就来一顿这样的夜宴，轮着进村去取给养；可季节还没过完，厄运就追上了我们。

一个漆黑的夜里，我们照常聚会，号令落到了莱斯特和大七人的乔尔（Joel）头上。小男孩们睡熟后，我们取出绳子，把乔尔从窗口放了下去。然后我们把套索套上莱斯特，开始放他下去。

===Original===
It chanced that Graybeard had lain awake from toothache, and was at that very moment looking through his window, the curtain of which he had neglected to pull down when he retired, and he saw, slowly descending outside, two dark objects; they grew longer and longer, then they suddenly ceased to move. For an instant he felt a slight shock of fright; but quickly recovering, he gradually made out the form of two feet and two legs without a body. He sprang out of bed, threw open the window, and in a severe tone demanded, “What’s this! Who are you; what are you doing?”

Lester struggled frantically to climb the rope; we tried to help him, but a large knot caught the edge of the windowsill, and we could not lift it over, nor could we let Lester down, for one of the Seven had entangled his legs in the coil, and before he had extricated himself, it was too late to save our companion.

“Who are you?” again called Graybeard, grasping the boy by the trousers.

“It’s me, Lester,” replied the lad.

Seeing that the game was up, we gently let Lester farther down, and he entered Graybeard’s room through the window.

===Chinese===
碰巧灰胡子牙疼，一直醒着，正那一刻朝窗外望——他睡前忘了拉窗帘——看见两个黑乎乎的东西在外头缓缓下降；那东西越来越长，忽然不动了。一瞬间他有点发毛；但很快定下神来，渐渐辨认出是两只脚两条腿、没有身子。他跳下床，推开窗，厉声喝问：“这是干什么！你们是谁？在做什么？”

莱斯特疯了似的往绳上爬；我们想拉他，可一个大结卡在了窗台沿上，怎么也抬不过去，也没法把莱斯特放下去——七人中有一个的腿让绳圈绞住了；等他抽出身来，救同伴已经来不及了。

“你是谁？”灰胡子又喊，一把揪住男孩的裤子。

“是我，莱斯特。”少年答道。

眼见把戏败露，我们把莱斯特又往下放了放，他从窗户进了灰胡子的房间。

===Original===
In the meantime one of the boys had run softly downstairs to open the hall door for Joel, who had not been discovered.

Graybeard woke us up in the morning at the usual hour, but of the disturbance during the night he said nothing. At breakfast the subject was not mentioned, although we listened with anxious expectation.

To the twelve boys who were engaged in the escapade of the night, it seemed as though the preliminary exercises of the morning school session would never end, so desirous were we to have the punishment, whatever it might be, come quickly and we be rid of suspense. The last name on the roll was called; Graybeard slowly closed the Register, put it in his desk, and during an impressive silence turned his eyes upon us to scan our faces.

“Lester!” said he, at last, “you will step up to my desk, if you please.”

If there was a serious matter on hand, Graybeard always said, “If you please.”

===Chinese===
与此同时，一个男孩早轻手轻脚跑下楼，给没被发现的乔尔开了大厅的门。

早晨灰胡子照常叫醒我们，对夜里的骚动只字不提。早饭桌上也没人提这事，尽管我们竖着耳朵提心吊胆地等着。

对参加夜里那场胡闹的十二个男孩来说，早晨的课前演练仿佛永远没个完——我们太盼着惩罚（管它是什么）快点来，好卸下悬着的心。点名点到名册最后一个；灰胡子缓缓合上点名册，收进课桌，在一片威严的静默中把目光转过来，扫视我们的脸。

“莱斯特！”他终于开口，“请你到讲台这儿来。”

凡有正经事，灰胡子总要说“请你”。

===Original===
Notwithstanding the very polite invitation extended to him, Lester reluctantly walked to the desk. Every eye but two, those of Alexander, was fixed upon Graybeard and Lester. Aleck had taken out his writing-book and was carefully copying the example given at the head of the page, “Honesty is the best policy.” He took particular pains with the capital *H*, finishing the last part with concentric circles.

“What were you doing last night,” asked Graybeard of Lester, “when I caught you outside of my window?”

“I was going down to the ground.”

“Were you running away?”

“No, sir.”

===Chinese===
尽管接到了这么客气的邀请，莱斯特还是老大不情愿地走到讲台前。除了两只眼睛——亚历山大的——所有的目光都钉在灰胡子和莱斯特身上。亚历克掏出了习字本，正一笔一画照页首的范例抄写：“诚实为上策。”那个大写的 H 他写得格外用心，收尾用的是一圈套一圈的圆。

“昨晚我在我窗外逮住你的时候，你在干什么？”灰胡子问莱斯特。

“我正要下到地上去。”

“你是要逃跑吗？”

“不是，先生。”

===Original===
“Where were you going?”

“I was going to the village.”

“What were you going to the village for?”

No answer.

“Who was letting you down; someone must have held the rope in the dormitory, who was it?”

===Chinese===
“你要去哪儿？”

“我要去村里。”

“你去村里干什么？”

没有回答。

“是谁在放你下去；总得有人在宿舍里拽着绳子，是谁？”

===Original===
No reply.

“If you don’t answer my questions, I shall have to whip you; who else was going with you?”

Lester looked appealingly to Brush, then to Alexander. Aleck was writing the sentence in his book; but, when he heard Graybeard’s threat to whip Lester, he arose without finishing the last word. All eyes turned upon him, and there was a stir among the pupils.

“What is it, Alexander,” asked Graybeard, “what do you know about this strange performance?”
